Nicholas No. Nein. Nay. C’est impossible. You see, if you’re planning on making any formal statements regarding your position here, I feel like I need to have someone else in the room. Standard protocol, you know. Just in case you were about to say something of great weight. In case the whole process of your transfer gets held up while whatever you’re about to say is investigated. It might temporarily put a stop on your transit, is what I’m saying. So. In light of that, what is it you would like to discuss with me today, Sam?

Sam (pause) Um . . .

Nicholas Sam? Anything?

Sam (pause) The, um . . . (Beat.) I think we should, uh . . . en – enforce an office policy of turning our machines off. Not just on standby. At lunch. And meetings, to set an example. You know? I think that would be a good idea . . .

Nicholas (smiles; points at him) For you, Samuel? Anything. (Pause.) Thanks, guys. If that’s everything –

Sam Oh, and there was a message for you from the selection committee. Your candidacy for local councillor is under review.

Nicholas (laughs) What?

Sam On the grounds of prejudice and discrimination towards a colleague.

Nina Sam, what are you doing? Is this about all of us?

Sam No, just him. Rumour has it that he, as my line manager, advised me to seek work in another department on the grounds that I was ‘a big queen’.

Nina What?

Nicholas He’s lying. Thanks, Sam, all right. Who’s ‘reviewing my candidacy’?

Sam The deputy fund-raising and members’ chairman for the Putney Association.

Nicholas Well, they don’t know what it was we spoke about do they, you fuck. It’s just your word against mine, because they weren’t there.

Sam Yes, they were.

Nicholas How were they?

Sam Because I was there.

Nicholas And?

Sam (unfolding a letter) And I’m the new deputy fund-raising and members’ chairman for the Putney Association.

Pause. Nicholas takes the letter and reads.

Sam You were right, Nick, with your reference I was able to get any job I wanted. Thanks for that.

Nicholas . . .

Sam Course, they thought I was grossly over-qualified but with the incumbent taking immediate leave and what with the selection for new councillors currently in motion they thought I’d better start straight away. My first job is to assess the suitability of candidates. I’m fine on three of them, there’s just a black mark against one. A claim of misconduct, doesn’t look good. (At Nina and Robbie.) Of course it will have to be qualified by other colleagues.

Nicholas (beat; claps his hands) Well bully for you, Miss Fucking Marple.

Nina Nicholas?

Nicholas I didn’t do anything, OK, Nina?! He’s making it up! Come on. Rob mate, you’re not going to go along with this, are you? He’s lying.

Robbie I dunno. You are the guy that asked what my sexuality was on my first morning.

Nicholas . . . (gasps in disbelief) . . . W – what? That was just. . . (Looks at Nina.)

Nina And I just think you’re a bit of a cunt.

Nicholas (pause; looks around; laughs; stops) Sam, come . . . Sammy . . .

Sam doesn’t flinch. Silence. Nicholas nods his head. Grabs his stuff. Pause.

Nicholas Just remember. That I said those things . . . because I wanted to help you, Sam. Because I wanted you to do well. Please at least remember that . . .

He leaves. Silence. Nina and Robbie break it, laughing.

Nina Shit the bed, Sam! I can’t believe that. And what’s this new job, deputy members’ –?

Sam Oh, it doesn’t matter, I’m quitting soon.

Robbie You’re what?

Sam Yeah, just wanted to make sure the right people made it on to the shortlist, then . . .

Nina And then what? (Excited.) You staying here?

Sam Nah, I think it probably is time to move somewhere different, you know.

Robbie I hear the Prime Minister’s position might be vacant soon. You could do that.

Sam (laughs) Well. I’d need to be an MP first.

Ted appears, standing at his desk with the card drawer open. Tim and Frank nearby, watching. Ted takes out one of the cards. . .

Nina Well, become an MP then, idiot. You’d be ace.

Sam Mmm, dunno. You kind of need more experience first. Like . . . I don’t . . . (Smiles knowingly.) A local councillor?

Nina For Wandsworth?!

Sam Ah-ah-ah. Cards close to your chest in this game, Neen. (Looks over to Ted.) Cards close to your chest. . .

Ted looks at Sam. He smiles, and rips the card, tossing it into the air and exiting with Tim and Frank as music grows. . .

Ray, Tommo, Michelle, Adam, Muznah, Shayne and

Heather enter, dancing over to Sam and tossing their own cards into the air.

Lights fade down.





Sons of York





Cast List


Sons of York was first performed at the Finborough Theatre, London, on 5 September 2008. The cast was as follows:

Mam

Colette Kelly



Dad

William Maxwell



Jim

Barry Aird



Mark

Steven Webb



Brenda

Kazia Pelka



Director Kate Wasserberg

Decor Alex Marker

Costumes Jenny Lee

Lighting Tom White

Sound Andrea J Cox

Musical Director Leigh Thompson





Characters


Jim, forty years old

Brenda, wife of Jim, thirty-nine years old

Dad, father to Jim, seventy-four years old

Mam, mother to Jim, seventy years old

Mark, son of Jim and Brenda, seventeen years old





Setting


Winter 1978–79. The living room of a family home in Hull.





Notes on the text


The play is written in an East Yorkshire dialect. When the word ‘the’ is missing from a sentence, it is usually indicated with a t’ – which should not be pronounced but remain silent.

Other notable distinctions include:

wan’t for ‘wasn’t’

din’t and in’t for ‘didn’t’ and ‘isn’t’

dun’t for ‘doesn’t’

A slash symbol / indicates that the character with the following line should begin speaking at this point.

(Text in brackets) indicates a line is spoken internally, or sotto voce.





Act One




Scene One


Early December 1978.

Dad’s house, Arthur Street, Hull. A living room. A door leads straight out on to a side yard. Another leads to a hallway and stairs, another to the kitchen. On a sideboard or dresser are models of AEC lorries and a framed photo of an AEC Mandator G4.

Jim stands with his coat on. Staring at Mam. She sits, head down, attached to a colostomy bag hanging from its apparatus. A dog starts barking outside. Dad shouts.

Dad (off) Argh, go’wan, out of it! Get! Oy, I’ll have you, yer little . . . Gowaaaan! Out! Stay!

He enters from outside, dumping some bags. He watches Jim watching Mam.

Dad Can talk to her, you know. Still hear you.

He leaves. Jim watches Mam. The dog barks outside.

Dad (off) Oy! Right, that’s it, yer little bastard! I mean it. Go’wan! Out! Oh gi’over, Mark, he’ll not bite. Knows who you are. Come on.

He enters the doorway with more bags, spinning back to shout outside.

Oy, don’t stroke him, though! Flamin’ ’eck, he’ll have yer hand. (To Jim.) Bloody. Gorra death wish, lad o’ your’n, or what?

Dad dumps the bags. Something smashes. Dad and Jim exchange looks.

Dad What were that?

Jim shrugs, mumbling ‘I don’t know.’

Dad Glass, or summat?

Jim Dunno, do I?

Dad Bringing glasses for? All this stuff, / can’t stay down here.

Jim I know, Dad, I did tell ’em, / just . . .

Dad Back room, have to be; / your room.

Jim Din’t know how long / we’d be staying, did we?

Dad Still, we’ll be all right, not we? Few weeks. Have a laugh, whatnot, eh? (Louder for Mam.) Ay up, Val, they’re ’ere. (To Jim .) How’s Aggie? All right?

Jim (shrugs) Yeah, Dad, fine, you know. Just . . .

Dad (referencing Mam) Said hello or owt yet? Still bloody hear you.

Jim (looks at his Mam. Struggles) She . . . ?

Dad ’S up?

Jim She all right? Like.

Dad Yeah, ’bout same. Why, when’d you last see her?

Jim Dunno. (Shrugs.) (Last week, or . . .)

Dad You what?

Brenda and Mark bundle in from outside with the last of the bags. Brenda is in her nurse’s uniform. Mark has a battered-looking acoustic guitar.

Brenda Phew! That’s last of it, Terry.

Dad That it?

Mark (sarcastically) Don’t worry, / Dad, we’ve gorrit.

Brenda Yep. Eeh dear, look at it all. Sorry, Terry, not / a place to stand now, is there?

Dad (taking the guitar and having a strum) Hee-hee, look at this. Jimmy Hendrix, ’ere.

Mark (putting a box down) Ow, me bastard finger.

Jim / Mark. Don’t.

Dad (louder) Ay up, Val, everyone’s here.

The general activity and noise stops as the new arrivals look at Mam. Beat.

Dad Our lodgers for over t’ winter and what have you. (Laughs.)

Brenda (beat; louder; moving to her) You all right then, Valerie?

Dad I say, road’s all right wi’ diversion?

Jim Yeah, just had to go down Bev Road / and, uh –

Brenda (to Mam still) Owt doing?

Jim – and back across on Anlaby. Ten minutes door to door / still, so . . .

Brenda Let’s take a look at you then, shall we? Ooh, cold today, eh, Val. Brr.

Dad Ay, it’s Hull Fair weather, in’t it? (Laughs, nudges Mark.)

Brenda (rubbing Mam’s hands) Feel them hands, / need to warm ’em up, don’t we?

Dad Hull Fair weather.

Mark Is / heating on, Granddad? Freezing.

Brenda God, do you know, I’d kill for your hands / when I’m your age, Val. Look at them compared to mine already. People’d kill for them. They would.

Dad (to Mark) What’s that? Eh, come on, sit yersen down, lad. Not staying or what?

Jim Course he is; we are. Mark, sit.

Dad I say, Brenda, she’s quiet today.

Brenda When d’you last get her seen, Terry?

Dad Ah, thee always say t’ same thing, you know. ‘Call if you need.’ ‘Gi’ yer bit of help.’ (Know what that means.) But, course, they don’t see her when she’s right, you know. They only . . . well, you know, see her when she’s under t’ weather. ’Cause o’ course that’s why I’ve called ’em out, in’t it. So, it – you know, it – you . . .

Brenda She had her tea today?

Mark / (We an’t.)

Dad Gorra bit down her. Normally stick it in fridge, case she wants it later on, like.

Brenda Mark, come and say hello to your Nan.

Dad (wiping nose with handkerchief) Or / I’ll have it, you know.

Mark (loud) Hello, Nana. / Y’all right?

Jim You gorra cold, Dad?

Dad As I say, quiet today. Yesterday, couldn’t shut her up, but . . .

Brenda She is a bit on the parky side, Terry. Got / to keep her warm.

Dad Her head, an’all, Bren. That keeps dropping forward, bit. / Is that –

Brenda Oh yeah. (Lifting her head.) Is it just when / she’s like dosing, or . . .

Mam (groaning from being touched) Urgh . . . my . . . what yer / der wi’ me . . .

Brenda ’S all right, Val, OK, just giving you a little checkup. Nodded off there, didn’t yer?

Mam (looking around) . . . Erm . . . urggh . . . (Tapping her head to think.) Where me, uh –

Dad ’S that, Val, all right?

Mam Shwhere me, uh, yuh . . . erm . . . (Shakes her head.) Nope. Gone. (Laughs.)

The others laugh politely along with her (Dad most), except Jim, who seems to get caught off guard, but joins in with a smile when he notices the others.

Brenda Say, I’ve got a portable kit in the car, Terry, tests blood pressure.

Dad What’s that?

Brenda Little monitor thing for blood pressure. ’S in t’ car. May as well, eh, while it’s here.

Dad Oh. Test her blood pressure eh? (Oh.) (Beat. Looks at Mark.) Bloody hell, hardly see you there, lad. Nowt on yer.

Mark Ge’ orr, I’m all right, Granddad.

Dad (jabbing playfully at his arm) Look at that, knock you over. Come on, hands up.

Mark (his hands up, laughing) Gerroff. ’Bout knock me over? Wouldn’t last a round.

Dad (back and forth, jabbing) What me, heavyweight champion of Arthur Street? Cor.

Mark Yeah, an’ I’m heavyweight champion of Humberside.

Dad stops, and cracks him round the back of the head.

Mark Ouch, / all right! That for?

Dad Oy! None of that swearing in this bloody house; on about ‘Humberside’. Day I recognise that bloody Tory creation, ’s day I start doin’ Lambeth friggin’ Walk. We’re Yorkshire’s, what we are, nowt else. That’s what thee bloody wanted. Cut us off. ’S only reason thee building us that bridge, make us feel like we’re not bloody forgotten.

Mark Eh, we caught sight of it other day, on way to Hessle, massive now. Huge. Middle bit of road meets ’tween them two legs. Towers. Things.

Dad Ay, well, certainly make a difference, give it that. Eh, Jim? And you, Mark, once you start. Crossing over there, ’stead of having to go all round.

Jim ’Bout fifty miles off, thee reckon.

Dad Ay, make it easier gerrin in and out of this little city of ours. Eh? (Jabbing Mark .) Ey, Marcus lad? Look. Telling yer, see, old man, get you to the ground, no problem.

Mark (laughing) Bollocks, could you.

Dad gets Mark into headlock as they begin to play-scuffle.

Dad Yeah, see, look, look at that then, eh? / No bother. Cor. Come on, little pansy, gerrin!

Mark Yeah, all right then. I’ll . . . ow, oy, all right, gerrof, Jesus!

It looks almost as though it will get out of control as they tumble across the room, Mark breaking free and fixing his hair.

Dad All right then, bloody hell. What, I an’t messed it, have I? Were a mess anyway.

Brenda He’s trying to do that spiky thing thee have now, but he can’t get it to stick.

Mark I can! Just takes time. / Needs to grow, dun’t it, and . . .

Dad (laughing) Spikey thing?

Brenda Got my side’s hair, I’m afraid. Stubborn as a mule.

Dad Factory all right? Oy, how’s yer lessons going, driving?

Mark Uh, OK thanks, Granddad, yeah.

Dad We wanna be getting you out, dun’t we; out on the road wi’ Aggie. Only forty-two payments left now, you know.

What’s that, Jim, sixteen years since? Year you were born, actually. Your dad already wi’ me at Tooley’s, Johnny just started. (Grabs the photo from the side.) ’S when we decided, all on us, on our dear old Aggie. (Look at her.) Like big girls, Mark? (Laughs.) Firm expanding, see, new fleet. Opportunity for a co-operative. I thought ‘I’ll have that! Yep’. Stake in a lorry. Every bugger in yard laughing, well, who’s pissing themsens now, eh? Forty-two payments left. Soon as you get cracking, that halves, two a month, ’stead of one. Not bad, eh, lad of your age, part-owning a truck, bloody Nora.

Brenda Mark, you thanked your Granddad for putting us up yet?

Mark Yeah. Yes. Thank you, Granddad.

Dad Well, that’s all right, that’s OK, thank you. Makes sense, dun’t it, heating one house ’stead of two, what wi’ everything up in’t air, fuel, food. Gonna have to bunker down aren’t we, get ready for the fight! Ho, ho. I’m looking forward to it, me, should be fun, eh? Mark? Altogether? Say, Ford back at work now, Jim. Victorious, pay rise in hand. See? That’ll be it now, flood gates. Ford were, um, you know. The what’s it. Thingy what comes first, leads the way. (Oh God. Go to the files.) Horse.

Jim Thee reckon us lot’ll be out after Christmas, Dad? Entire T&G.

Dad Ho, ho. See. (Claps his hands.) Ye-es, it’s coming. Biggest yet, I can feel it. Like ’26. Downing tools, altogether. Government, ‘no pay rises over five per cent’? Bloody . . . dunno how thee dare. Well, they’ll, they’ll . . . see, exactly. After Christmas, say?

Jim Yeah, New Year, reckon.

Dad ’Bout time, ’cause whatever they offer you, not be more than five, ’less Hell freezes – or should I say Hull freezes over. Ha ha. Eh, which it will, tellin’ yer. Bring this city, country, to a standstill, we can. That’s the . . . ’Bout your lot Bren’, what’s the chat?

Brenda No one’s said owt, Terry. Royal College of Nurses aren’t exactly the IRA.

Dad Yeah, but a general strike is / . . . well, means everyone, dun’t it, or . . .

Brenda Folk are always gonna need nurses, whether . . .

Dad Well, ay up, shall we have a brew, then start getting this lot up? / Take tea, Brenda?

Jim Come on, Mark, start gathering.

Brenda Lovely, Terry, thank you.

Dad (to Jim) Jim, tea?

Jim Ay, go on then.

Dad Tea, Mark?

Mark No, ta, Granddad.

Dad Uh?

Mark I’m all right, thank you.

Brenda Oh, just have a cup of tea, Mark, will you, Christ’s sake?

Mark All right, then, yeah.

Dad Yes?

Mark Yes, ta, Granddad. / (To Jim.) Ay up, what is sleeping arrangements, anyhow?

Dad So tea, tea, tea. Sugar? (To Brenda.) Sugar, Brenda?

Brenda / No thank you, Terry.

Jim You’ll be in back room, wi’ us.

Mark You what?

Dad Sugar, Jim?

Jim No thanks, Dad.

Dad Mark? Sugar?

Mark Three please, Granddad.

Mark/Brenda Mark.

Mark No thanks, Granddad.

Dad No?

Mark No, ta. Ay up, an’ all, am I really gonna have to share a room wi’ these pair?

Dad Why, how many rooms do you think we’ve got? ’S not Buckingham Palace you know. You’ll be right on floor, got cushions and blankets. So, three teas, no sugar.

Dad heads to the kitchen, mumbling ‘Three teas, no sugar’.

Mark Right, well, I don’t want you two gerrin’ up to owt.

Jim slaps him round the back of the head.

Dad (off) Ay up, we can make it Irish. Ha. Got that Scotch, Jim. What’s it? Glen doodah. (singing.) ‘A you take the high road, an’ I’ll take the low road, ta dee, ta la da, la . . . ’

Brenda is putting on elastic gloves to check Mam’s colostomy bag. Dad returns.

Dad (to Jim) Ay up, reckon ’bout trip to Rayners, pint or two, tomorrow evening, or nip into town, Black Horse? Pick up pasty and chips, way home? If / Bren’ not mind –

Brenda Bag’s a bit full, Terry.

Dad Eh? Is it?

Brenda (loud, to Mam) Nice and full, this, Val, eh? Good colour.

Dad Is it?

Brenda Mark, come here.

Mark What?

Brenda (unclipping the bag) Take this.

Mark Fuck off!

Jim slaps him, back of the head.

Mark Ouch! What?! / Bloody . . .

Jim Do as you’re told.

Mark Not touching that.

Brenda Oh grow up, Mark, you little git, it’s only urine.

Mark Where am I emptying it?

Brenda Where d’you bloody think? Toilet, upstairs! Daft ’aporth.

Dad Had some trouble wi’ it, mind. Flush. Keeps brekkin’.

Brenda (offering the bag to Mark) Here.

Mark (taking it, arms length) Fuck’s sake.

Brenda/Jim Mark!

Mark I’m going!

Dad Should be all right now, though. (Watches Mark cross the room.) Ay up, Mark.

Mark What?

Dad You taking the piss?

Dad, Brenda and Jim (who can’t help himself) guffaw into laughter. Then Mark.

Mark (laughs) Yeah, yeah.

Brenda Aw, is everyone picking on you, love?

Mark ’S all right. Used to it, aren’t I?

Dad (still laughing) Taking the piss . . . ha ha ha! Aw dear . . .

Brenda Not your fault, is it love? Born breached, weren’t you?

Mark Ge’orr, will you. Let me empty this, if I’m going.

Brenda Arrived backwards. Arse first into the world.

Dad Ay, well, it’s no wonder he talks out of it, then, in’t it?

They all laugh again. Loud, leg-slapping. Mark heads upstairs with the bag.

Brenda Always remember him ’bout fainting, Terry, reckoned his baby had two heads.

Dad Two heads! Ha ha ha. Ay up, bet you must have thought, ’ere, what’s the crack?

All burst into hysterics again.

Brenda (laughing, to Mam) ‘What’s the crack,’ Val? (Stands, makes for the stairs.) Aw, stop it, you buggers, I’m gonna wet mesen.

Dad (still laughing) What d’yer do when you saw . . . a-hu-hu . . . saw that sticking out?

Jim Nowt, I just, uh-uh . . . ay up, I just turned the other cheek!

All burst into more hysterics.

Brenda Aw God, I’m gonna have to go. ’Bout burstin’. (Exits at speed, still laughing).

Dad (wiping his eyes) Aw, dear dear. Ha ha ha . . .

They laugh into silence. Dad sits by Mam. He notices something in her hair and brushes it. He tugs at her clothes, straightening them. Mam winces and shifts. Dad takes out a pipe and tobacco and fills it. The kettle in the kitchen begins to simmer. . .

Dad Heard from your brother?

Jim Uh . . . no . . . (not, uh) . . .

Dad Out today?

Jim Uh, yeah.

Dad Up Brid, was it?

Jim Uh?

Dad Bridlington?

Jim Uh, no. Hudders . . . it were Huddersfield today. Brid were, um . . . Brid were Tuesday.

Dad Oh. (Oh, Huddersfield.) What drop?

Jim This, uh . . . just this, like, decorating place. Pallets of paint, you know.

Dad My last run, you know, Brid.

Jim Yeah.

Dad Ten year, now.

Jim (Yep.)

Dad Go M62 Huddersfield?

Jim Uh, no. Traffic / were bad, so I went –

Dad Straight across, in’t it. / Hour and half?

Jim No, traffic were murder, Dad, so took ’62 out on to t’ M1 then across on A638 down Dewsbury to / pick it up –

Dad Dewsbury? And then –?

Jim Yeah, then picked up / M62 near Brigham –

Dad Yer go past Brigham, then?

Jim Then off, yeah, then round on to A640, and that takes you as / good as in.

Mam (mumbling, loudly) Ma ba my, my . . . where’s . . . schjonny, me . . . why / is he me . . .

Dad He’s just popped down the shops, Val, back soon, OK?

Mam Sche back wha? John, where me . . . (Patting her pockets.) Wi’ me schthings, and –

Dad Yeah, Johnny’s got all your stuff. Back in a tick, eh. Shh. All right. (All right.)

Mam grumbles into silence.

Dad (pause) A6?

Jim – 40.

Dad Oh. (Oh. A640.) Good run?

Jim Were all right.

Dad Two?

Jim And a bit.

Dad Oh right.

Pause. Jim chuckles.

Dad What?

Jim Taking t’ piss.

Dad chuckles.

Jim Were good, that.

Dad Your lot playing Craven Park, tomorrow? Robins?

Jim Ay. Reckon it’s ours this year; top o’ league.

Dad We’re top o’ league.

Jim Yeah, League Two, Dad.

Dad Still bloody top o’ league, in’t it? Not lost a game all season. Be up wi’ you, next year. (Pause. Smokes his pipe.) Good road, 1035 Brid. Running along by t’ sea. Nice bit of sunshine. Nice bit of breeze. Window down. Easy curves, quiet road. Glad that were me last ’un. Last drop. Always enjoyed that road.

Dad holds Mam’s hand, looking at it. The kettle begins to whistle. Jim looks towards the kitchen, then at Dad. He makes to go and turn it off as Dad stands, heading into the kitchen. Brenda and Mark return from upstairs.

Brenda Phew. That were close. Need a bag o’ me own soon, crikey. You all right? Quiet.

Jim (shrugs) (Dunno, just . . . you know, just . . .)

Brenda (looks at Mam; back to Jim; quiet) Gunno have to . . . well, talk about it, some point. I hadn’t realised she’d gotten . . . well . . . so different. So soon.

Jim Well, it . . . (Pause. Watches her. Nudges her with his hand.) You all right, though?

Brenda Yeah. I’m all right. Long as you’re all right.

Mark (rolling a fag at the table) I’m all right an’ all, incidentally.

Brenda Oy you, outside with that. In fact, not at all.

Mark Why, he’s gorr’is pipe, an’t he?

Brenda Mark, would yer . . . ! Don’t be a gobshite today, please. Nuff on.

Mark Oy, I’m one what’s got stuff needs doing. Dunno why we can’t be at ours.

Brenda (referencing Mam) You bloody know why. Now, off your arse, / help with this.

Jim On about ‘stuff needs doing’ – what stuff?

Mark Work stuff. You know. (With air quotes.) ‘Factory work’.

Jim grabs Mark by the collar and pulls him in.

Mark (taken aback; quiet) What . . . ?

Dad returns from the kitchen. Jim lets go of Mark.

Dad Just stewing. Shall we get some of these up then, come on. Set to. (Looking at some of the records.) Garr, look at all this. Your music, Mark? All shouting and screaming.

Mark They’re angry, Granddad.

Brenda Aw, don’t get him / up on his soapbox, Terry, he’ll never come down.

Dad Angry, what thee angry about? Not living on rations, are thee, not seven-day weeks just to make do and mend, eh? On about angry. Not wearing rags, are yer? (Singing.) ‘And so I’ll go, from rags to riches.’

Ha ha ha! That was what’s-his-face. Thingy.

Jim Elvis.

Dad No, not bloody Elvis, thingy who lives up Kirkella. Used to make breezeblocks. David.

Brenda Whitfield.

Dad Whitfield. There we are. Whitfield. Sang with him, both did, oh yes. In the clubs. Uh, Newington – no, Tivoli. Tivoli Theatre. Course he had a go with the best of ’em, that boy. Bing, Frank, thingy-what’s-it. Not like this groaning they do now, bloody Elvis Presley, Billy thingummy-you-like, Jim. Gaarh.

Mark ’S all finished now, Granddad, we’ve gone back to proper music like what you had.

Jim That were proper music.

Mark (singing) ‘No Elvis, no Beatles, no Rolling Stones’.

Dad Good, get shot of the lot of them.

Mark and Dad exit up the stairs with bags. Brenda holds Jim back.

Brenda Oy. Not wanna spend a bit of time with your Mam? Go on, I’ve got these.

Jim What? No, I’m / . . . It –

Brenda (takes his bags) Look ’ere, go on. Have five minutes.

Brenda exits. Jim turns to Mam. He saunters over. He watches her. Beat. He sits next to her. Beat. He lifts his hand as though to take hers, but brings it down again. He looks around the room. Without looking at her, he slowly takes her hand in his.

Jim begins to struggle; stop himself from breaking. Bringing his clenched fist up to his mouth. Shifting around in his chair. Looking anywhere but at his Mam.

He covers his eyes with his free hand. The sound of the sea creeps in softly, as the peripheral lights of the room draw in. Jim calms. The faint sound of the fairground, as Jim and Mam are soon alone, bathed in a warm pool of sunshine. Jim lays his head back, resting the light on his face, as Mam turns her head to look at his hand.

‘I’m Gonna Sit Right Down and Write Myself a Letter’ by Vera Lynn begins. Mam turns away from Jim, and sings. Jim, a world away, begins to fade. . .





Mam


‘I’m gonna sit right down, and write myself a letter

And make believe it came from you . . . ’

She stands and approaches an old-fashioned, free-standing silver microphone, as the rest of the room fades and she’s alone in a spotlight.

‘I’m gonna write words so sweet, they’re gonna knock me off my feet,

A lot of kisses on the bottom, I’ll be glad I got ’em.

I’m gonna smile and say ‘I hope you’re feeling better’,

And close with love, the way you do,

I’m gonna sit right down and write myself a letter.

And make believe it came from you . . . ’





Scene Two


Evening. Dad and Mark are by a record player, playing chords on the guitar. Brenda sits with Mam, awake and smiling. Jim is at the table with a blood-pressure kit, the pad around his arm. They’re all eating fish and chips. Dad plays an ‘A’ and laughs.

Mark (arranging Dad’s fingers on the guitar) Then. E.

Dad (plays) Argh, bugger it.

Mark No, that were right. And . . . (Re-arranging.) C.

Dad (plays) Ay.

Mark There you go, them three chords, A, E, C. / Anyone can play, that’s the point.

Dad plays all three chords.

Brenda Chuck us vinegar, Jim.

Jim Next to yer foot.

Dad (finishing the guitar) Hey-hey, ’s good this, Jim. What d’you reckon?

Mark plays ‘Career Opportunities’ by The Clash on the record player, and begins jumping up and down, singing. Dad laughs, joining in with the guitar.

Mark (singing)

‘They offered me the office, offered me the shop,

They said I better take / anything they got,

Do you wanna make tea at the BBC?

Do you wanna be, do really wanna be a cop?’

Jim Oy, Mark. Calm down.

Brenda (clapping along) Do you like this, Val?

Mam (laughing) Ooh-hoo-hoo . . .

Brenda You done wi’ your fish? Look, ’nuther bite maybe. Come on.

Mark (singing)

‘Career opportunities are the ones that never knock

Every job they offer you is to keep out the dock,

Career opportunities, the ones that never knock!’

Dad Eh, what’s all that he saying then? Career opportunities? / Down the docks?

Jim Mark, off. Hear mesen think.

Mark (turns it off) It’s just about the whole thing, Granddad, how we’re bloody, frigging stuck in a rut and can’t afford no houses and job opportunities rubbish and just all t’ stuff our Mum and Dad’s had, with their money and flowers and hippy peace shit in t’ sixties we an’t . . . mean, it’s – we’re more like your lot, Granddad, not taking no shit.

Dad (laughs) Eh, this is marve – I like – Eh, ’s better than what you listen to, Jim.

Mark Nah that’s all music to play in a sweet shop, Granddad. It in’t rock ’n’ roll. All bobbing your head from side to side and skipping round like pansies.

Mam (laughing) Wi’ him up and sch-schinging, / and . . . and . . . hoo-hoo. Yeah, yeah. Um . . .

Dad Yeah, ’s right Val, remember?

Brenda How’s / your blood pressure, Jim?

Dad Well, I’m glad to see you’ve got musical streak in yer, lad.

Mam And shwell I neber. Well I, I never.

Brenda Yeah, / well I never, Val.

Jim ’S not working. Just says ‘error’.

Brenda (going over) Error? / Have a look. Not best time to do it, eating chips, course.

Dad Say, your father, course. Completely tone deaf, can’t sing a ruddy, a, a bloody note, you know. Eh, you should do a few what’s-it. Gigs, like. Get out there.

Mark Uh, yeah. Well, actually, I’d been thinking / about, um –

Dad Course thee probably don’t have old circuit any more, eh? What your Nan and I used to sing on. Newington. Uh, what’s it? Dance hall. Locarno. Eh, we wouldn’t have met, weren’t for that circuit, / back in them days.

Brenda (taking the pad off) Works fine, Jim. Christ, 110 over 70. / That’s gone up.

Dad Val, remember? Beverley Road baths. You and Kingston Communication Girls?

Mark Swimming?

Dad No, back in them days, might still . . . used to put a floor, wood floor over t’ pool, board it up, and have dances and that, dead popular. And she were there on stage singing wi’ the girls. I hadn’t started sing – (Louder.) I hadn’t started singing that point, eh, Val. You know, public. But you were, on t’ stage. The Vera Lynn of Ryde Street, eh?

(Singing.) ‘Pennsylvania six five thousand.’

(Laughs. Eats his chips.) Course, after t’ war, I come back, and I’m like, ‘Where’s Hull gone? Half of it’s bloody missing.’ But more theatres up here than in West End before that. Come back, that’s gone, and – (indicating Jim) my baby boy over there’s now bloody eight, walking, bloody talking. And then your uncle were born. Still managed to get out there, bit. Eh, Val? Oh yes.

Dad takes Mam’s hands in his and swings them as he sings. Mam laughs.

Dad (singing)

‘When a man has a son, like my son John,

He’s a man who can smile when he talks,

And the eyes have a sparkle,

And he walks with his head held high . . . ’

Mam laughs and claps her hands as Dad leaves her, laughing, dancing.

Dad Aw, there were some greats what sung there, kiddo, Bev Baths. Tommy Fisher and his band. And, eh, ’parrently Louis Armstrong himsen popped in, 1930s sometime.

Mark What? Bollocks.

Dad ’S true, ooh ay.

Mark What were Louis Armstrong doing in Hull?

Dad Dunno, do I. Passing through.

Mark You can’t pass through Hull, ’less you wanna get wet.

Dad Mark. Cheek.

Mark Eh, you know Mike Ronson were born up Bev Road, Granddad. Him in Spiders from Mars, David Bowie. / Amazing, in’t it.

Dad Who? Oh ay, yeah. / Local lad, that band.

Mark Can’t believe he were in Uncle John’s year at school! Fucking love them. /

(Singing.) ‘Ground control to Major Tom.’

Dad Well, Johnny probably played wi’ him, like. Used to like a sing. Pair o’ lungs on him. (Nearly at the end of his chips, reading the newspaper.) Oop, look Jim, ’ere – oh, same as what we read yesterday (Hull Daily Mail). ’Bout BP and Esso drivers striking. See? Petrol stations be empty, few weeks. Though makes me wonder, way some report it, on telly and that, you wouldn’t think it were workers exercising their right, fair pay for a fair day’s work. ’S like they’re all coming right-wing again. Oh yes. It happens, telling yer, I’ve seen it before. Used to believe we were a nation of Labour voters who’d now and again get scared and edge right, but now I’m starting to think it’s other way round, and deep down we’re just a nation full of bloody Tories.

Mark Reckon Labour’ll be out next time, Granddad?

Dad Nah, they just need remembering their roots, dun’t thee? What they stand for.

Brenda (standing and starting to clear) Ay well, we din’t even vote for this guy, did we – just got in when other ’un left. Ask me, / ’s about time . . .

Dad Well, got to say, expected more of Callaghan. Union man. Mean, I know it’s backs against wall time, economy buggered, but it’s not – you’re not even demanding t’ world, are you? Just a return to collective bargaining. Unions and employer working out pay, not bloody government stopping this and saying that. ’S bloody . . .

Mark But in’t that what Maggie wants, her lot?

Dad Ge’orr, wi’ you, lad! Talk to me about her; gladly see the back o’ working man. No, have to preserve this lot no matter what, because if we don’t, others’ll be in. And folk, they’ve short memories. They forget. Well better a, you know, like a struggling Labour government than them back, any day, oh yes. / Oh yes!

Mark I know, just saying. Aren’t they into freedom and business and / enterprise and –

Dad Freedom and –? Oh ay, no, they always say, said it last time, them rich bastards at top do well and it all trickles down. Well bugger it, ’s never trickled as far as Hull.

Mark Maybe that’s why they’re building t’ bridge across Humber.

Jim Mark.

Dad (pointing at him) Yeah, you joke. ’Cause I’m warning you. Bloody . . . all this. You wait, they get in, and all this . . . wait and see. ’Cause it’s you gorra live with it, not me. But we’re surviving, us lot. Just. By t’ skin of our teeth. Eh, Jim? Enemies at our door, drawbridge is up, Keep’s gotta hold. But we’ve been here before, oh ay, ’26, General Strike. (Louder.) Eh, Val? ‘Not a penny off the pay, not a second on the day!’ Still, you’ll not mind, eh, Mark, not having to go to t’ factory. Ha ha. Bloody blessing, I’ll bet. You can join me and Dad down’t Docks.

Mark Huh. Can’t imagine me dad wi’ a placard in his hand, chanting.

Jim Eh, I’ve done my share.

Mark What, when you missed that bus? (Laughs.)

Dad (laughs) Ha ha ha!

Jim Yeah, ha-bloody-ha. Who told – (Points at Dad.) That were him, I take it.

Mark What were it, Granddad, summet . . . ?

Dad It were T&– no, CND rally, Aldermaston. What year, Jim? Fifties? Big un. Gorr’im sen dressed up, made his signs, not normally like that, mind. All his mates going. Bus left outside Hammonds eight o’clock and he bloody missed it. (Laughs.)

Mark (laughs) Aw, Dad . . .

Jim It left bloody early, I were there, eight o’clock.

Dad Folk never let him forget it.

Jim (You bloody don’t.)

Brenda Ah well, ’s good job you did, otherwise you’d probably have been arrested.

Dad Argh, what’s that gorra do wi’ price of fish?

Mark Haven’t you ever done owt else?

Jim Marched wi’ dockers, and . . . eh, ’ark at you, what you bloody . . . ? There an’t been owt to flamin’ . . . you know, get all up about, has thee. Struck ’69, over t’ White Paper.

Mark Din’t join Rolling Stones in what’s-it Square, then? / Grosvenor.

Jim Look at ’im, on like I’m – had to bloody look after you, din’t I?! And there an’t been owt to . . . Oy, I’m bloody ’ere, aren’t I, now? Gonna be out on front line! Friggin’ . . .

Brenda All right, Jim. Settle down. Can’t we just watch telly or something?

Dad Gar, there’ll / be nowt on.

Brenda Dick Emery not on. / You like him, Terry.

Dad Oh, yeah we like him, though.

Mark Oy, do yer impression, Dad. That priest one he does.

Jim Nah, can’t be arsed.

Brenda / Oh, Jim.

Dad Eh, / like him, Val, don’t we?

Mark Or yer Tommy Cooper.

Jim No.

Mark Go on. (Imitating.) ‘Just like that.’

Dad (laughs; imitates) That Tommy Cooper? ‘Just like that.’ Ha ha. ’S good, that.

Mark I prefer Kenny Everett to Dick thingy.

Brenda What’s that one he does? (Imitating.) ‘Oh, you are awful.’ I like that.

Mark I like – (Imitates, leg crossing etc.) ‘But it’s all done in the best pah-sibble taste!’

He looks hopefully at the others, who exchange ‘unsure’ glances in silence. Beat.

Brenda (clearing up the papers) Everyone done / wi’ their rubbish?

Dad Arh, stick it on then, if you want. Maybe catch sports round-up. Hoo-hah! Nuther win for the Arlie Birds! Ay up, Mark, you picked your team yet, or what?

Mark (still digging round his chips) Nah, Granddad, I’m not really into it, rugby –

Dad Come on, lad, ’s only a bit of fun. Eky, and don’t just go pickin’ Rovers, ’cause of your father. We’re in West Hull, ’member, that’s F.C. territory.

Mark Dad dun’t really support Rovers.

Jim I do.

Mark Yeah, but not really, really –

Dad Ah, ’cause it’s only bit o’ fun, in’t it? We picked out a hat, Jim, din’t we? When they were little. Just to make t’ pools bit more interesting. Ended up me and Johnny got Hull F.C. and your dad and yer nan, Kingston Rovers. Din’t mean owt then, as I say, just made weekly pools more interesting, different teams. ’Cept Wembley, ’59! Oho-ho, your dad wan’t half green. Me and Johnny went down, cup final, Wigan.

Jim Yeah, and Rovers were Wembley final, ’64, Widnes.

Dad Yeah, you didn’t bloody go though, did yer?!

Jim Couldn’t afford to bloody – and had to work! Better game, though! Better than yours!

Dad Ah, what’s it matter?! Both bloody lost, din’t we?

Mark (laughing) Have either ever won owt?

Dad Course thee . . . what’s he on wi’?

Mark I bet if Hull played Hull it’d still somehow end up that Wigan won.

Jim / Dad (exchanging glances) We’re top o’ league!

Jim Dad, you’re League Two!

Dad Still top o’ league, in’t it?! Bloody hell!

Brenda Doing pools next week, Terry? Or we gonna try and cut back a bit?

Dad What? ’S only few pence, ge-orr wi’ yer. Just a bit of fun. If we din’t put in and turns out coulda won, you be sore then, wun’t yer?

Mark Y’ever won owt?

Dad Bits and bobs, here and there. (Sneering.) And course your dad and Val’s ‘famous win’. Which we still don’t know what thee spent it on, but there y’are.

Jim You never wanted to bloody know! He were right, jealous.

Dad And I still don’t wanna know; / I’m not bothered.

Jim Were only a couple of quid.

Mark When were this?

Dad Normally me and Johnny what did well, mind. Then thems two sneak in wi’ a little win. Bloody big-headed for days, he were. I said, ‘Just go off and spend it! I can’t be doing wi’ yer!’ And whatever it were, thee came back end o’ day, smile on their face which, Mark, were, shall we say, an infrequent occurrence, your dad, that age. Well, nowt new appeared in t’ house, so thee must have frittered it away on summat silly. Unless there’s a mansion somewhere in Beverley I don’t know about.

Jim Dad, I’ll bloody tell yer if you want.

Dad No, I don’t bloody care. (Nowt to me. Did what you want.)

Jim chuckles, as suddenly Mam stands shakily and starts shuffling out. Brenda and Dad leap to her aid, grabbing the colostomy bag apparatus.

Dad Whoop, / steady there, steady.

Brenda Whoa, all right there, Val. Just going for a little walk, are you?

Dad Ay, she’ll be right, probably just thinks it’s time for bed. Ay up, which it is. Here.

Brenda Phoaw. She shot up quick, didn’t she? / Blimey.

Dad Ay, that’s thing, if she wants to get somewhere, she’ll get, like. I’ve gor her, now.

Brenda Sure she’s all right up them stairs, Terry?

Dad Oh ay, we always manage. Come on, Val, up we go, there we are, that’s it . . .

Brenda Mark, help your nan and granddad, will yer? Sat / there like a lump.

Dad Nah, we’re all right.

Jim Mark. Up.

Dad and Mam exit, shuffling up the stairs. Followed by Mark.

Brenda looks at Jim.

Brenda (sighs) Jim.

Jim What?

Brenda You know what. Look / at her –

Jim What. She’s right.

Brenda He seems to think that this is just a . . . a temporary thing, that she’s gonna get better. Or stay the same. And she isn’t. Jim. It’s gonna get worse –

Jim He not . . . Bren, you know what he’s – he’ll not listen, he’s –

Brenda You don’t know, he might be waiting for someone to say summat, offer help. Jim, cupboards were bare; he’s not eating, so she’s not eating, it’s North Pole in ’ere –

Jim Bren, I . . . Please, I can’t . . .

Brenda Look, I know it in’t easy.

Jim (moving away, shifting around) (Please . . .)

Brenda Jim, I know. All right? I do know.

Jim struggles, rubs the back of his neck, doesn’t look at her. . .

Brenda (taken a bit aback by how upset he is) Jim . . . ?

Jim D’yer ’ark at him, eh? Smug little . . . gi’in it all working-class solidarity.

Brenda You what?

Jim When tomorrow he’ll have his nose back in a bloody textbook.

Brenda (pause; warmer, now) Aw, what’s up? Were they taking Mick out on yer? (Moving into a hug.) Well. I like Elvis. And I like the Kinks. And I quite like you, sometimes. Come here. (Hugs tighter.)

Jim Hmm. (Sighs. Pause. As Sean Connery.) Ah, Mish Moneypenny. I mush be dreaming.

Brenda chuckles. Mark thumps back down the stairs and they break. Jim sits back down and puts the blood-pressure pad back on.

Mark So what we saying about tomorrow, then?

Jim ’Bout what?

Mark ’Bout where I’m gonna be. He thinks I gonna be at factory, dun’t he?

Jim Awh, look. No, what’s it matter, sixth form or factory, you’re out in the day aren’t you? And he not bloody know. It in’t time now, what wi’ . . . It in’t time.

Mark Never is, is it?

Brenda September were the right time, when he started.

Jim I know, Bren! Yes. Should have just said. Straight out. I know, but she . . . (Indicates Mam.) What wi’ everything just . . . starting, I . . . I just couldn’t find a –

Mark I don’t even think he’ll mind, much.

Jim Are you soft? All he’s been going on about. Forty-two payments left. Forty-one –

Mark I can’t help it if I don’t wanna drive lorries, Dad. If / I want summat else to –

Jim Aw, I know, all very DH bloody Lawrence, in’t it? Flippin’ –

Mark Christ, it in’t even like it’s – Half lads I know have stayed on, it’s just what you do now. And Mam got her training, bloody Uncle John upped and left, so why all of a sudden is it up to me to –

Jim Fine! Well, you bloody say summat then! Gi’ him summat else to . . . bloody – (Rips the pad off his arm.) Argh, this bastard thing, it in’t working.

Mark (sits, puts the pad around his arm) ’S cause you need a pulse to check pressure.

Dad returns from upstairs.

Dad Oy, what’s shouting? (Pause.) Just having a lie-down. Better today, though, eh? Than yesterday. (Looks at Mark with arm up, elbow in table.) What you up to, there?

Mark Just testing me blood pressure, Granddad.

Dad (sitting) Thought you were wanting an arm wrestle, hand up like that. (Takes his hand.) Grr. Come on then, let’s have yer.

Mark Ge’or, you’ll mess up me reading.

Dad Come on, you can do better than that, young lad like you. (Wrestling. Slams it down.) Urh! There. Blood and stomach pills, lad, your nan could do better. No upper-arm strength, that’s your problem, same wi’ your dad. No strength in his arms. ’Cause all the drops are unloaded wi’ fork-lifts now, of course. Not handballing like what we had to do back then. (Imitating the tossing of boxes.) One, heave, two, heave. Grrr. (Claps his hands and offers his arm again.) Come on, then, one more.

Mark (standing) Nah, I’m all right, Granddad, you’ve hurt me arm.

Dad Have I bollocks. Come on, sit. Not leaving this table till you beat me.

Jim Just sit down, Mark. Christ’s sake.

Mark sits. They arm wrestle, Mark beginning to really try.

Dad (laughing) That’s it. Eh up, look, he’s goin’ red. Come on. What’s the matter with yer? Eh up, referee, look at that. Elbow off table. Disqualification, that is, come on.

Mark stands now, serious, really pushing down, shaking. He uses both hands.

Dad Cor blimey, up on his feet, and he still can’t take me down. Look at that.

Jim Oy, Mark, calm down.

Dad (standing) Nah, he’s all right, if that’s how we’re playing, look. Cwooar. . .

Dad starts pushing Mark’s hand the other way. He grabs Mark by the back of the neck and starts pushing his whole body down on the table. Mark yells out a bit.

Brenda Oy! / Go easy, both on yer. Watch that monitor.

Dad Yeah, you having that, are you, having that? This how we’re playing, is it? Little cheat, are you, Mark?! Have to cheat to win, eh?!

He wins, and raises his hands on a triumphant walk. Mark lying on the table.

Dad There we are, there we go. Undefeated. Eh by gum. Phew. (Claps his hands together.) Right, Jim. Up next.

Jim Nah, I’m –

Dad Semi-finals. Winner plays me. (Sits Jim down.) Mark, come on. Father versus son.

Mark No, I’m all right actually.

Dad Oy, stop whining like a little girl, it’s only a bit of fun. Winner gets my jumbo sausage, how about that?

Mark Well, it in’t fair, I’m coming off the back of three bouts / and he’s fresh as a –

Dad Oh, will you shut your cake hole, Mark, and just wrestle your bloody dad. Go.

They arm wrestle, Dad cheering them both on, as the room begins to fade.

‘Who’s Taking You Home Tonight?’ by Vera Lynn plays. Mam appears at her microphone. Dad sees her, and steps forward, out of the scene as it fades to black.

Mam (singing)

‘They met at a dance, in a waltz of romance,

Strangers, but strange as it seems,

The girl’s heart was thrilled, her dream was fulfilled,

As these words, from the boy, of her dreams . . . ’

Mam moves towards Dad, and they hold each other, beginning to turn.

Dad (singing)

‘Who’s taking you home tonight,

After the dance is through.

Who’s going to hold you tight,

And whisper, ‘I love you, I do . . . ’





Scene Three


Afternoon. Jim is at the table, his back to Mam, gluing pieces to a model ship. A completed ship in a bottle sits nearby. Mam is pale, covered in a tartan blanket. Jim opens a lunchbox and begins eating a sandwich. He never looks at her while talking.

Jim Meant to be for me lunch. This snap. And on my what’s it . . . my clock, it’s, like. Well. Breakfast, at moment. That’s problem, Mam. Overnight jobs. Can handle t’ sleep thing. Ne’er been a problem. It’s food what gets yer. Gets me, any road. Body still wants lunch at lunch and tea at tea. This is me eating me lunch at what should be, on my clock, me breakfast. But I already ate me breakfast. At real breakfast. So. Now I’ll have to eat my ‘real-time’ tea at ‘my-clock’ lunch which means when it gets to ‘my-clock’ tea, ‘real-time’ breakfast . . . (Chews, thinks.) I’ll have run out of food.

He finishes his sandwich and gets back to his model. It distracts his speech flow. . .

Royal Sovereign, Mam. Really, uh . . . dead. Intricate, like. Piddly little thing, but prefer these. Dunno why, really, just . . . like ’em. Royal Sovereign were one what fought at Trafalgar. ’Cause Nelson’s plan, like, were to have our ships in two horizontal rows what pierced through French vertical line, meaning French top half had to sail back round to get their broadside, side wi’ guns, back facing us again, leaving us time to concentrate on their rear. Well this ship, captained by chap called Collingwood, were head of one line, and Nelson, in Victory, them’s other. And it were Sovereign what reached enemy line first, splitting it. Had a copper bottom. See? But this Collingwood, he never got credit what Nelson did. Weren’t as flash, you know. Weren’t as . . . dunno, just . . . had a, a . . . a measured mind. And a quiet. Courage. You know. And it’s that what . . . (what, uh) . . .

Brenda enters from outside with bags of shopping, followed by Mark, helping.

Brenda How do? Look at this stray child I picked up off the street, way back from school.

Mark (College.) Eh, Dad, guess what?!

Jim Mark! Have to shout all t’ time, always shouting.

Brenda Oh, leave him. / All right, Val? How are we? (Feels Mam’s forehead. Sighs.)

Mark Guess who came into school today?

Jim The teachers?

Mark Only Philip fuckin’ Larkin. (Laughs.)

Brenda Jim, I’ll drop you off at yard on me way round to Infirmary.

Mark He’s librarian up at Uni. Read one of his poems out. Was the bollocks, man.

Jim And does this Philip Larkin, poet and wordsmith, use language like what you do?

Mark No, he uses much worse.

Brenda Ten minutes, all right? Can’t be late today.

Jim (turning, seeing the shopping) Lot there.

Brenda Jackson’s was full, folk just taking things, panicking, thought I better gerrin quick.

Jim Ay up, you gonna hear owt from your lot today? What thee been saying?

Brenda They’re not saying much, Jim. No one bloody knows what’s gunna happen.

Jim But if thee do call you out, though? (Waits.) Bren’?

Brenda Then we’ll see, not we.

Jim What do you mean, ‘we’ll see’? Bre-en . . .

Brenda I mean, I’m a nurse. Jim. And mother. And Wife With House And Kid And Two Elderly Parents-In-Law. / Is what I mean.

Jim Ay, eh, eh, I’m just . . . he’s gonna expect you to be here, in’t he?

Brenda And why? For ‘solidarity’ or because he expects me at home to look after . . . well . . .

Jim That in’t fair. Bren. / And this is his house. If he –

Brenda I don’t work wi’ trucks, Jim. It’s people. Yes, and we’re here to help him. / Not . . .

Jim And if factory you’re meant to be at goes, Mark, he’s gonna expect you to be here.

Mark Well I can’t be, I’ve got classes. Exams. / We’re just gonna have to tell him.

Jim Oy, aren’t you wanting to get involved anyway? Bloody student, / thought you’d be –

Mark Not if it means failing!

Jim And your teachers won’t cross picket line anyway, will thee? / Public servants.

Mark And if we don’t now, bloody when? New Year? Easter? After I’ve passed me exams?

Jim If you pass your exams.

Pause. Mark grabs the shopping bags.

Mark Thanks, Dad. (Exits into the kitchen.)

Jim (off Brenda’s look) What? What do you want me to do, eh?

Brenda Nothing, Jim. Bloody nothing. (Exiting.)

Jim Bren, if you don’t tell what’s gerrin on your wick, I can’t bloody . . . can I?

Brenda exits with the bags. The dog starts barking outside. We hear Dad yell it to be quiet. He enters with a lead and a newspaper. Mark and Brenda come back in.

Dad How do, how do. Good days, all? (Louder, kissing Mam on the forehead.) Back, Val! Well, I say, Jim. Everyone. It’s all going on now. Swung by yard, wait till you get down there. Had a word with Smithy, word is entire T&G being called out, 3rd of Jan.

Jim 3rd of Jan?

Dad Everyone, haulage drivers, private, public, trains, buses, even ambulance. Tools down from 3rd of Jan. Spread across whole country. This has been brewing. It has. And I’ll tell you summet for nothing, he’ll wish he’d called that election when he had the chance, oh yes. Bloody hell, ’cause if he had, he’d have scraped in, just. But now, ho! (With the paper.) See. Here, calling it ‘Winter of Discontent’. Get ready.

Mark Richard III, that.

Dad Eh?

Mark Shakespeare. ‘Now is the winter of our discontent, / Made glorious summer by this son of York.’

Dad Be your lot an’ all, Bren, surely. ’Bout you, Mark. What’s chat at work? Owt new?

Mark Uh. I got promoted today, Granddad.

Dad You’ve what? What d’ya mean?

Mark (glaring at Jim) Been promoted. To hot dogs.

Dad Oh. Oh. Hot dogs. What were you on before?

Mark Uh, I was on fish paste before. It’s like the lowest of the low. Me. Pete. Danny with the gammy leg. Russian Mike. But they liked what I was doing on the ol’ paste front, see, so I’ve leapt up the ladder. To hot dogs.

Dad Well. Eh up, don’t get too comfortable there, lad. Remember it’s only part-time.

Mark They say if I keep my nose clean, get me head down, like, they reckon one day I might make it all the way to turkey drumsticks –

Dad’s back is turned. Jim gives Mark a jab in the arm.

Mark Oww . . . Was your day, / then, all right?

Dad Turkey drumsticks, eh? Blimey. Ooh, what were that . . . (Thinks.) Ooh, go to the files, the fish, urgh, awful fish, around after the war, only thing you could bloody get.

Mark / Tuna?

Dad She’d know. (Loudly.) Remember. Val? No, not . . . It were, like, canned / summat –

Mark I hate tuna.

Dad Really oily, like. Snoek! That were it. (To ‘the files’.) Thank you. Big tins of Snoek. Yurk. Remember that. Horrible. Horrible fish. Oily it were, yurk. Turkey drumsticks, then, eh? Well, what thee saying your place? What union you wi’?

Mark Uh. Same as dad. Transport and General.

Dad Who’s shop steward, good man? What’s his name?

Mark It’s, um. Er, Don.

Dad Don? Don what?

Mark Don. John.

Dad Don John? Don’t know him, Labour man? No Johns in t’ club, I know of.
