Act Two




Scene One


Mark is perched on a high stool, tuning his guitar. He adjusts the height of the microphone. Speaks out to the audience. He is charmingly relaxed, and at ease.

Mark All right? Thank you for. Coming. Just . . . (Tuning his guitar.)

(To audience members coming in,) ’S all right, come in, sit down. Not started yet.

(Tuning. Tests the mic.) One, two, one, two. Can yer hear that? (More strumming.)

All right. Ladies, gents. Hello. Ta for . . . coming back. Main event up next, I’m just, um . . . I’ve got to try and warm you up. Which is, given bloody temperature, near but impossible. Huh. Come round our house, icicles hanging off gas fire. Not even lying. Any you lot got folk on strike? (Looks around.) Yeah? Bit mad, in’t it? Anyone from Hessle in tonight? (Waits.) No, I’d stay quiet an’ all. Any way.

(Clears his throat.) Uh, right, this . . . uh, something I’ve been working on, so . . . go easy, yeah? Erm. Basically, not, not to apologise for it, like, but what wi’ lights going on and off and gloves on your hands, what have you, it’s not quite as . . . (tunes) quite as ready as I’d have liked, but . . . it is what it is, so . . .

He begins to play the opening bars of his song. He stops. Pause. Less at ease. . .

Actually, sorry, do you know what . . . (Pause.) I . . . (Pause.) How about this?

(Plays. Sings)

‘There was a young man from . . . ’ (Laughs.)

Nah, only. Joking. Erm. (Pause.) Man walks into t’ doctors wi’ a strawberry on his head. (More tuning. . .) Says, ‘Yer got any cream for this?’

Christmas crackers, eh? Bloody . . . um, any road, how about one from, uh . . . well, I think, one of the most beautiful women in the world, Ms Patti Smith. Rock on, erm . . . and, uh, it’s, just to say, feels a bit mad me being here ’cause, uh . . . me granddad and grandma, they used to do the rounds. Singing. Round here, so . . . all a bit mad. Erm. Anyway. Patti Smith. ‘Elegies’.

(Plays. Singing.)

‘I just don’t know what to do tonight,

My head is aching as I drink and breathe

Memory falls like cream in my bones, moving on my own.’

Christmas lights, over in the darkness of the living room, begin to flicker on and off. The faint, tinny sound of Vera Lynn’s ‘The Love Bug Will Bite You’ emerges, upbeat and jazzy. Mark notices, but carries on. . .

‘There must be something I can dream tonight,

The air is filled with the moves of you,

All the fire is frozen yet still I have the will, ooh, ah.’

Dad hops on into the semi-darkness of the living room, lighting candles, humming along. The song too loud now, Mark, stops, stands and leaves. The main lights in the room flicker on. Dad smiles, blows out the candles and exits. The music fades.

‘Day of Action’: 22 January 1979. Afternoon. Jim enters from outside in full winter garb, snow about him. He stamps his feet and leans a placard against the wall, closes the door and stamps his feet. Brenda can be heard slamming about in the kitchen. Jim notices the Christmas decorations gathering around him.

Jim (Oh gawd, what’s he doin’?) Bren!

Brenda enters, wearing an apron, and a face of thunder.

Jim What you gorra face like a wet weekend for?

Brenda You think this is funny?

Jim No, Bren. I don’t.

Brenda Christmas dinner on Christmas Day, I bloody hate. Christmas dinner when it in’t even sodding Christmas makes me want to stab someone, Jim. And I’m not talking wi’ a sharp knife, I’m talking like a rolling pin. Blunt, and slow. Not that / there’s a sharp knife that whole kitchen.

Jim All right, Bren, I get the picture.

Brenda And when I can’t get hold of food and the lights keep dying and the bloody gas and the bloody water take it in turns to decide not to work –

Jim Right. Where’s Mark?

Brenda Are you listening?

Jim Yes. Where is he?

Brenda Jim!

Jim BREN! . . . (About to launch into something, but stops.)

Brenda Upstairs. His exam tomorrow. Well. ‘One assumes.’ Wi’ no teachers teaching, not exactly been best week for him. And I’m not sure what your father thinks he’s doing, locked in the room, but quite frankly if he finds out, he’d prefer him to be doing what he thought he were doing.

Jim Ay, well.

Brenda ’S it like outside?

Jim Mad. Nowt’s working. Roads are a nightmare.

Brenda Well, gritters are on strike, aren’t thee. (Like every bloody . . .)

Jim (wafting the air) How long’s that rubbish been out now? Starting to . . .

Brenda Two weeks. Move it away from t’ door if you want, got hands, an’t yer?

Jim (sighs, chucking his car keys down) That’s last of t’ petrol.

Brenda Eh?

Jim Car died.

Brenda You gerrit home?

Jim Couple streets away. Managed to pull up on t’ pavement at least. (Sits to take off his boots.) Good job they’re bringing her, otherwise . . .

Brenda It in’t a good job, Jim, it in’t / a good job at all –

Jim I know – Bren, I know. That’s not what I . . . (Looks about and sighs.) Dear, dear. Didn’t think he’d actually put decorations back up. (Touches the Christmas tree lights, gets a shock.) OUCH! Yer . . . bastard . . .

Brenda All right? (Making for the kitchen.) Better get back to it.

Jim What meat we havin’?

Brenda Nowt at moment.

Jim Eh?

Brenda Nowt at moment. You dad’s gone out to try and find something.

Jim Find something? What do you mean?

Brenda Shop. Neighbours. Lost child wandering the street.

Jim Can’t have veg on Christmas dinner wi’ no meat.

Brenda Makes you think you’re getting veg? And it in’t bloody Christmas.

She exits back into the kitchen. The Christmas tree lights flicker off again. Jim waggles the wire and gets another shock. Blackout.

Dad enters a spot as the music returns, singing ‘The Love Bug Will Bite You’.

Dad

‘Listen all you people, you better beware,

Love’s an epidemic, and it’s in the air,

It’s a thing that gets you even though you’re very smart,

So take some good advice and go and vaccinate your heart.’

Mam enters a spot at her mic, continuing the song.

Mam

‘The love bug will bite you if you don’t watch out,

If he ever bites then you’ll sing and shout, you’ll go – ’

Dad mimes the short solo with an imaginary trumpet.

Mam

‘That’s what love is all about.’

The living room lights up again. Jim enters, trying to start a fire in the fireplace.

Mam

‘You can’t eat, you can’t sleep, you’ll go crazy.

You’ll just lardi-dardi-dah all day.

If someone wants to know why you’re crazy,

You’ll answer – ’

Dad (Gibberish.)

Mam

‘The love bug will bite you if you don’t watch out,

If he ever bites then you’ll sing and shout, you’ll go – ’

Dad mimes the short solo with an imaginary trumpet.

Mam

‘That’s what love is all about.’

Dad and Mam jitterbug. In the living room, Brenda lays the table in a strop.

Dad

‘You can’t eat, you can’t sleep, you’ll go crazy.

You’ll just lardi-dardi-dardi all day.

If someone wants to know why you’re crazy,

You’ll answer – ’ (Gibberish.)

Dad / Mam

‘The love bug will bite you if you don’t watch out,

If he ever bites then you’ll sing and shout, (Gibberish.)

That’s what love is all about.’

The music comes to an end. Dad and Mam kiss, and disappear.

Mark comes stomping down the stairs with a book.

Mark Fuck’s sake. Heating’s off again.

Jim Need chuck more stuff on t’ fire.

Mark Well that’s no good for me upstairs, is it? Time’s she back?

Brenda Half an hour or so, home phoned. Surprised anyone’s available to bring her. See? He dun’t mind folk at the Home turning up to work, crossing t’ picket line, does he, oh no. But a nurse, at a hospital, with / patients, well . . .

Jim I know, Bren. It’s just today. It’s a national Day of Action, for fu – for pity’s sake. Mam coming home anyway, can’t we just keep him / happy –

Brenda She shouldn’t be coming home, Jim. / After all that –

Jim I know! Look! It . . . Home’s not good enough, there’s / staff on strike, things breaking –

Brenda Home is good enough. This home in’t good enough, he’s just trying to find fault. All that trouble and pain and gerrin over it and now we’re back to / square one.

Jim What do you want me to do? Eh?! Money’s run out, / there’s nothing –

Brenda And why has the bloody money run out? Is it because folk like him intimidate folk like you to stop working?

Jim Jesus, Bre – One out, all out, for fuck’s sake. Otherwise it / doesn’t bloody work.

Brenda It is different for us, Jim, our situation / is different.

Jim We all deserve better pay, Bren! Ever heard of a thing called socialism?! Bloody –

Brenda Don’t you bloody socialism me! All right! Don’t you bloody dare! Where is it that you think I work, Jim, where? Place where whether you’re rich, bloody poor, male, female, union or a pissing strike-breaker, you bleed the bloody same, die the bloody same. On about socialism, writing it on your little placards as a slogan. Right? I bloody know what it is. What you will have to explain to me is what that’s got to do wi’ standing on someone’s neck, shouting, ‘You will be free, you bugger, / or else.’

Jim Right, OK, ta for that party political broadcast from the Brenda party. When’s dinner?

Brenda You’re only not working yersen cause of what he might say.

Jim Bren.

Mark You don’t even believe in it, Dad, so why / don’t you . . .

Jim What? (Pause.) What did you just say?

Brenda Jim.

Jim What did you just say?! Come here!

Mark Well do you? Just ’cause Granddad does, / dun’t mean –

Jim And who you, eh? Big man. Say summat like that to / me? Who are you?

Brenda Oh Jim, please, let’s just get through today.

Jim What do you want me to do, eh?! You two?! Bloody laughing at me one day, ‘Urgh, missed fucking bus, kind of activist are you, ner-ner-ner.’ I’m just trying to do what’s best. And trying to keep him happy, right? What do you want me to do?

Mark (sighs) Can’t be down here long, Mam, you know, I’ve got –

Jim You’ll be down as long as your granddad wants you.

Mark Dad! Do you want me to fail? For some stupid . . .

Jim It’s Christmas, Mark.

Mark It’s the 22nd of January!

Jim It’s Christmas for your nan! Look! / I know it in’t . . .

Brenda If Mark needs to go upstairs and study he needs to study –

Mark / Thank you.

Jim He’s not –

Mark Can’t we make summat up, I dunno, like I’ve to go out, factory have called me in.

Jim Oh yeah, strike-breaker, that’ll make him even happier.

Mark Well, I’m not staying down here / all night!

Jim LOOK! (Pause. Calmer.) Look. Can we . . . can we just keep him happy? For now. For one day. All right? This is what he wants. This is his house. We’re his family.

Mark (Yeah, we’re your family an’ all, but . . .)

Jim Eh?

Mark Nowt.

Jim EH?!

Dog barks outside. Dad enters, holding a plate wrapped in paper.

Dad All right, Jim. Here you go, Brenda. Bits of chicken, already cooked. See?

Brenda (taking it) Already cooked, where from?

Dad Jean, yesterday’s Sunday roast. Wrapped it up, out of habit, for t’ boy’s sandwiches, like, forgetting they’d not be working. Already cooked, just needs warming up.

Brenda Right. Thank you.

Dad Been up at docks, Jim? Pwoar, bet it were what’s it, charged today, eh? Busy?

Jim Yes, Dad, very.

Dad Nowt open, you know Bren. Everywhere closed, hardly anyone about. Million they reckon, today. Maybe million and a half. Tools down. Incredible.

Brenda I know. Emergency’s only at Infirmary. (Exits into kitchen.) ’Ever that means.

Dad (stoking the fire, chucking some kindling on) Brrr. Bit on the chilly side, eh? All right there, Mark lad? (Seeing the book.) What’s that?

Mark Nowt. Just. Found it.

Dad Well, let’s have it, then.

He takes the book from Mark and tosses it on to the fire. Mark stares open-mouthed, watching it burn, as Dad arranges two table chairs next to each other.

Dad (sitting) Come on, then, lad. Lesson time. Let’s see what you know.

Mark Yer what?

Dad Come on, got to keep your brain in gear, what wi’ pumps empty, you don’t want to get out of practice, do you? Come on, climb into cab. Close door, bloody freezing.

Mark wanders over and sits.

Dad Right. (Miming.) Seat belt on. Click. Come on.

Mark mimes, unenthusiastic.

Dad Check your mirrors are all set. Oop, mine’s a bit off, unclick, window down, lean out. There, better. Window up. Seat belt on. Click. Yours OK?

Mark Yep.

Dad You sure?

Mark Yep.

Dad Right. ‘All right, Aggie, how do? Had a good sleep?’ (Laughs.) Engine on then.

Mark ‘turns the engine on’.

Dad Come on, lad, ABC, accelerator, brake, clutch. You’ve got one on brake and one dangling bloody nowhere. Right. What gear you in?

Mark Dunno. Gear stick’s invisible.

Dad Well, come on then, bloody dawdling. Pull away. Gently. OK. How fast we going?

Mark Not very?

Dad Is that because handbrake’s on?

Mark Oh yeah. (‘Lifts handbrake’.)

Dad Right, gently. Gently. OK, stop at main road. Handbrake back on, always just in case. These things are so heavy you’ll roll uphill. Now. Which way we going?

Mark Dunno. Wherever.

Dad North, south, east, west? See, that were the thing wi’ me. I were what’s called a tramper. Every drop somewhere new, every day summat different. Your dad, he’s a trunker. Same drops roughly week on week, couple of monthly jobs. Course, I grant you, different wi’ me, your grandmother were at home looking after the boys, whereas your mam, she works. In fact it were your nan what looked after you, lot of the time, while your mam . . . well, anyway. Like I say, sometimes you’d be out for days, stuck places waiting for a return load, see. ’Cause empty trucks on the road, well, they’re not earning any money, are thee. But you’d get to know other folk who’d kip at truck stops. And bed and breakfasts like. (Laughs.) And I’m telling you, some of these landladies. Notorious, thee were. No-torious. Doncaster Donna. Big Guns Bradford. Polly ‘Poke’ in Stoke. (Laughs.) Course sometimes, and I don’t advise you this, but you’d try and make it home, rather than stay over anywhere. Meant the odd fiddle of yer log sheets. Well, you have books now, but . . . you know, you’d learn tricks. Just got to try and keep awake, you know. Hot flask of something. Window down. One time I had this CB radio thing. Course your dad’s put one of them cassette things in Aggie now. Though Lord only knows what he listens to. Anyhow. You keep at it, son. Not long now. Forty-one!

Dad laughs and stands. He fiddles with a video recorder and TV as Brenda enters.

Brenda Mark, help me set the table.

Dad Ay up, works a treat, this video what’s-it. Got everything on she’ll have missed. First video recorder on Hessle Road, when Bill gorrit. Din’t reckon much to at time, like but . . . ’Specially if she missed it. (Watching Brenda.) Eh, I . . . I am awful grateful, you know, Bren. I, I, I know it’s a bit of a pain in the arse, nuther Christmas dinner, all that, but . . . I just, she would have so enjoyed it, like, and . . . well, / you know –

Brenda You’re . . . you’re all right, Terry. It’s . . . (Taking some rubbish outside.) ’S all right.

Dad Eh you must be chuffed though, Mark, second Christmas. Cor! Every boy’s dream.

Brenda (popping her head round the front door) She’s here. Van’s pulled up (Exiting.)

Dad Whup! Right-ho.

Dad exits. Mark puts crackers out on the table. Noise of doors slamming, Dad greeting Mam outside. ‘Thanks’ to the driver. After a bit, Mam is wheeled in.

Dad Here we are, Val, eh?! Home sweet home.

They look at Mam. She’s gaunt and weaker than last we saw her, oblivious to where she is, head to one side, eyes half closed. Behind Dad, Brenda shoots Jim a look.

Dad (looking for a response) Well, here she is. Jim, say hello.

Jim . . .

Mark (unsure) Hello, Nana. You all right?

Dad Come on, let’s get you in, then. And look. Decorations are still up and everything. And table set. We’re gonna have another Christmas Day, Val, ’cause you missed the last one, din’t you? Well, part from one you had in there but it wan’t proper, were it, not how you like it, any road. Here we are. (Wheels her to the table.) There. Nice bit of chicken, Brenda’s got, some veg. Lovely.

Dad hops to the video recorder while Brenda and Mark plate up. Jim watches. Brenda looks Mam over while Dad is distracted before going back in the kitchen.

Dad Ay up an’all, look at this, Val. Twentieth century now, whey-hey! Gorra video recorder, tape programmes what we like. First video player on Hessle Road when he got it. First one. Taped the Queen’s speech for you Val, ’cause I know we always like that, don’t we? Good one this year. That’s all on tape. Watch that. Eh, crikey, it’s a year and a half since jubilee, in’t it? God! (Clapping his hands.) Remember that? (Rearranging her clothes, straightening her out.) Out on the street. You remember that, Jim? Christ, never seen owt like it, streams of flags going from house to house, zig-zagging down the street. We’ve gorra plate, an’t we, wi’ that on? Scullery, somewhere. Eh, ’ark at me calling it scullery. Kitchen. (Calling.) Brenda? (Back to Mam.) Victoria sponge, that were yourn, oh my God, people were licking their lips for days, bloody days.

(Laughs.) Which you renamed Elizabeth Sponge, remember? And that’s . . . hold on, am I right, or . . . yeah, that’s right, in’t it? Thinking I were gerrin mixed up over VE Day then. Remember that, Val? Eeh, dear, some good parties though, we’ve had, eh? Can’t believe . . . I can’t believe just last Christmas you were . . . you know, up and . . . doing, erm . . .

(Singing)

‘I’m dreaming, of a white Christmas,

Just like the ones I used to know . . .’

Brenda (laying some veg, sighing) All right, it isn’t much, but here we are, tuck in.

Dad Whey. Grub’s up. Ta, Bren, thanks so much. / Look at this, eh, Val?

Jim Looks lovely, Bren.

Mark Ta, Mam.

Brenda Well, it’s turnips ’stead of parsnips and it’s Aunt Bessies, not me own, but / you’ve got your cauliflower and what-not . . .

Dad Ay up, / nowt wrong wi’ Aunt Bessies. Yorkshire gal, any road.

Jim ’S fine, Bren, honestly. Thank you.

Brenda Oh. Gravy. (Exiting to the kitchen.)

Dad Eh, and crackers as well. Crackers, Val. These new? These new, Bren, or ones we had?

Brenda (coming back in) No, new ones, one of the few things that were easy to find, and half price at that in Jan. Everyone all right, got enough?

Jim Yes, Bren.

Brenda Drink of owt? Water?

Mark Mum, honestly, it’s all fine.

Brenda Right. (Sits.) Sorry / we’ve no wine and that.

Dad (with the cracker) Eh, these are smashing, these. Shall we pull ’em now? Come on, Val, here you are. Everyone get pulling.

He gives his cracker to Mam, holds her hand around it, and pulls. The others watch this awkward manoeuvre.

Dad Whey! There, well done, love. Come on, everyone. Get cracking. Ay up. Cracking!

Brenda Come on though, tuck in eating while we do, it’ll get cold.

Dad laughs and pulls his apart while the others pull crackers between them. Dad puts a paper hat on Mam’s head.

Dad Ah, see. Purple. Your colour, Val.

Brenda What’s the joke, Terry?

Dad Joke?

Brenda In / the cracker.

Mark God, no more. / Please.

Dad Oh. (Searching.)

Mark / One round of shit jokes bad enough.

Brenda Jim, you’ve got one there, an’ all.

Jim doesn’t hear, watching Mam.

Brenda Oy, Jim. There you go. ’Ere Mark, pull this. (Pulls a cracker.) Whup. There we are. Where is it. Oh. Hold on. (Reading.) ‘What do you call a fish with no eyes?’ (Pause.) ‘Fsh’. (Laughs.) Fsh. Like that, Terry?

Dad stands and nips over to fetch back a Christmas card.

Dad Ay up, Val, and look at this. Guess who this is from. Look. (Opens the card.) ‘Merry Christmas, Mam and Dad. Love Johnny and Family, kiss.’ Eh? Nice, in’t it? Look. (Displaying the envelope.) Look at postmark. ‘Telford’. In Telford at the moment, Val. ’S nice there. Nice for the kids and everything. Not even that far away, is it, Jim? M1, A50, A518, job done. Ah, that were really nice of ’em.

Mark Say he’s still with Auntie Jane?

Dad ‘Love Johnny and Family, kiss’. Yeah.

Jim Dun’t say which family.

Dad ‘Love Johnny and Family’, Val. We know who that is, don’t we? Jane and the kids.

Brenda (pause) Come on, we’ve got cracker jokes scattered about table. Let’s hear some more.

Dad Here we go. Let’s read this, Val. Uh . . . (Reading.) ‘One piece of string . . . ’ What’s that?

Brenda (holding out a cracker) ’Ere y’are Jim, may as well use ’em up.

Dad (reading) ‘One piece of string to another: Are you a bow? No. I’m afraid not.’ Don’t get that.

Brenda (laugh) Oh, I like that one. Frayed knot, Terry. Like on a / shoelace.

Mark Don’t worry, Granddad, / they’re never funny.

Dad Like that, Val?

Brenda Ooh Mark, just bloody lighten up, will yer?

Dad Eh, we’ve taped Morecambe and Wise, an’ all. Christmas special. Were a blinder, wan’t it, Jim? Had that / uh, that comedy actor on we like.

Brenda Chicken all right, Mark?

Mark Hmm. Yes, Mam. Thank you.

Dad What were his name / Jim? Comedy actor.

Brenda / Right. Jim, read your joke.

Jim Leonard Rossiter.

Dad / Leonard Rossiter.

Brenda Oh, I’ll just read it. (Reading.) ‘Why did the man get sacked from the orange factory?’

Dad We like him.

Brenda ‘Couldn’t concentrate’.

Mark Eh, that Eric Morecombe looks / a bit like Philip Larkin, you know.

Brenda (No one’s listening.)

Dad Philip . . . ? Oh yeah, poet. From up, erm . . . what’s that one he wrote?

Mark (looking at Jim) ‘They fuck you up, your Mum and Dad’?

Dad (unravelling another joke) No, erm . . . Ooh, look, nuther one here. / ‘What’s the longest word in the English language?’

Jim ’S lovely, Bren. Dinner.

Brenda Well. It in’t lovely, but thanks for . . . / you know . . .

Dad (reading) ‘Smiles. Because there is a mile between the first and last letters.’ Eh?

Brenda Mark. Read that one.

Mark Do I have to?

Brenda / Jim Mark.

The phone rings. Dad stands, goes and answers it. Brenda reaches across the table and holds Mam’s arm. Their dialogue runs hushed under Dad’s on the phone.

Brenda All right, Val? You open your eyes for me, love? Jim, look at her.

Mark She OK?

Brenda Jim, she can’t stay here.

Jim looks at his Mam.

Brenda Jim. God, she’s freezing. Mark, wrap this blanket round her. Jim, have a word, you understand? We’ll have to call ’em, get ’em to pick her back up. (Waits.) Jim?

Dad (overlapping above, answering the phone) Hello?

How do, Sparky, what’s new, how’s tricks, you all right? . . . Do you wanna speak to Jim, or . . . ? Only we’re just eating, Sparks . . . No, we’re all ’ere. Where you calling from? . . . Oh ay. Right . . . You’re joking, never? What time? . . . Well, I’ll tell him but I don’t reckon we’ll be out at all today what wi’ everything. And Val’s just come home an’all, Sparks. From’t nursing home, like . . . Yeah she’s fine. I’ll pass on your . . . yeah . . . All right, well, have fun. Wish I were there. (Laughs,) All right, go well young man, take care. Ta’ra.

He puts the phone down and sits back down at the table, stroking Mam’s hair.

Mark Who were that?

Dad Sparky, from t’ yard. Says they’ve had a call from some of the lads still driving for, erm . . . what’s it, drugs place. Pharmaceuticals, you know. Says they’ve rang ahead, let them know where the drop is, one of them depots out towards the new bridge.

Brenda Phone ahead? Why?

Dad So pickets can come and stop ’em getting in, like. You know. From making the drop.

Brenda Well, if they don’t want to make the drop, why thee working?

Dad Well, it’s sabotage, in’t it? Working from t’ inside. Mark. / What’s your joke?

Brenda It’s so they don’t have to cross picket line but still keep earning. / (Cheeky gits.)

Dad Come on, they’re good, these crackers. / All right, Val. Good grub, eh?

Brenda Maybe Jim / could do that, eh?

Mark No, / Granddad, it’s shit.

Jim Bren.

Brenda Earn a bit of money, ’s been two weeks, and we’re struggling.

Jim Bren.

Dad Not struggling. Mark, go on. Stop being a daft sod. / Read it.

Brenda Not struggling? Terry, we’ve had to / take Val out the home.

Mark (reading) ‘What does a grape do when you – ’

Dad Because it wan’t good enough, Bren.

Mark See, / no one’s listening.

Dad Food and, and not washing her clothes properly, and just the way you / get treated, there. No. Oy, I’m listening, Mark. Read it.

Brenda I’m just saying, if there was a way to stay / on strike and still earn, Terry, then –

Mark ‘What does a grape do when you tread on it?’

Jim Brenda.

Brenda Jim! I’m / trying to find a compromise, here.

Dad Enough, right? Mark? / Finish the frigging joke.

Brenda I think she’d still be better off / there, Terry. I’m talking at someone who –

Mark It’s rubbish / anyway. Can’t we just –

Dad She’s better off here! At home! Mark! What does a grape do when you tread on it?!

Mark ‘Lets out a little wine.’

Dad Thank you!

He eats. Silence. Jim watches Mam.

Dad ’S up, Jim? Get eating.

Jim slowly carries on eating.

Dad (beat, stands) Come on. Gordon Bennett. Meant to be Christmas. Let’s have some festive tunes. Eh, Val? Bit of the old sing-song?

Dad plays ‘Have Yourself a Merry Little Christmas’ on the record player. Dad sings along, hopping back to his chair, holding Mam’s hand and prompting her along.

Dad Ho yes, like this one, don’t we?

Mark Ay up, if you want me and Dad to go join that picket, Granddad, I’d be happy to.

Dad No, that’s all right, we’re having our proper Christmas, aren’t we?

Jim Mark, leave it.

Mark I don’t mind. (Eyeballing Jim .) Popping out for a couple hours or so. On My Own.

Dad No, stay where you are, lad. They know what they’re doing.

Mark (pause) Say, new bridge open soon, though. Couple o’ year.

Dad Longest one in the world, it’ll be.

Mark Yeah, just a shame it joins up fuck-all with nowhere.

Brenda Val all right, Terry? She’s shivering.

Dad Yeah, fine, just gerrin used to being somewhere new again, in’t she?

Mark I were reading that, uh . . . it’s so fucking long – sorry – it’s so long. That engineers and what-not have had to take into account the curve of the earth when designing it.

Dad Curve of the earth?! Bloody hell, curve of the earth, Val.

Mark And. This is even better. All the cables that hold it up, like, off them two legs. There’ll be enough cabling there to go round the entire planet, twice.

Dad Go round the entire planet?!

Mark Twice.

Dad Twice?! No wonder it’s cost so bleeding much, eh? Eh, Val? All that money for a bridge. Need more than a pools win to buy that, eh?

Mark That what you spent yourn on, Dad? This new bridge belong to you and Nan?

Dad Oh, don’t bloody let’s get on to that. I don’t want to know. I Don’t Want To Know. (Pause. He eats.) Ay up, just thinking back, Jim. Well you probably can’t remember, just a kid when I got back after t’ war. But parties what happened in the street then. They were a bit like this, ’cause of course, folk couldn’t afford anything then, I mean really anything. Ay up, and that were summer that Labour got in, Mark. Clement Atlee, best government we have ever had! National Health Service, well, you know, Brenda. Care for anyone what needs it – a revolution, it were. Welfare, benefits for those what need it. Free comprehensive education. Nah, that’s real Labour, and don’t you forget it when you cast your first ever vote, kiddo. (Trying to feed Mam.) Come on, Val, ’nuther mouthful, eh?

The lights in the room go out. The music stops. Darkness, bar the fire embers.

Brenda Oh God . . .

Dad / Whoop. Lekkie’s gone.

Mark Jesus.

Dad All right, no bother. Don’t worry, Val, this has been happening regular. Where’s me –

He finds a torch and turns it on, giving more light. He opens the door to check outside. Brenda sets about lighting a few candles around the table and room.

Jim Is it fuse box, reckon?

Dad Nah, whole street’s gone. Eeh dear. (Closing the door.) No bother, light a few candles and we’ll be right, eh? Like Christmas in the olden days, eh, Val? Never off for long, is it Jim? Got to watch our Morecambe and Wise tape later, an’t we?

Mark Just popping upstairs.

Brenda Jim, can you do anything with that fire? Freezing.

Jim goes to stoke the fire as Mark slinks away. Dad tries to feed Mam. She groans.

Brenda (checking her out) Do you know what they were giving her there, pill-wise?

Dad Nah, just stuff to . . . you know, dope her up. Keep her quiet. Bah!

Brenda There’s that spare blanket in our room, I’ll just go and fetch it.

Dad No, she’s fine, Bren. Come on, we haven’t even finished dinner yet. Jim, sit down.

Jim Just gerrin fire going again, Dad.

Dad Fire’s fine, sit. Bren, please. Where’s Mark?

Brenda Think he’s just nipped upstairs, Terry, he weren’t feeling too good.

Dad Eh? What you on wi’? Looked fine to me. (Going to call.) Mark?!

Brenda Jim?

Jim What?

Dad Mark?! Get down here!

Mam groans.

Brenda Oh, Val. ’S all right. Come here. (Strokes her forehead.) I’ll get that blanket.

Dad She – Bren. Leave it. Jim, would you bloody sit?

Mark lollops down the stairs.

Mark What?

Dad What do you mean, bloody what? We’re having Christmas dinner, here. Sit down.

Brenda Terry, do you reckon we should maybe put Val to bed for a bit? She’s exhausted.

Dad What? No –

Brenda And then have a serious think about . . . (Sighs.) About what to do tomorrow.

Dad What to do tomorrow? What tomorrow?

Brenda About taking her back.

Dad Take her back? Take her – she wants to / be here. Val?

Brenda Jim, would you give me a hand?

Dad Stay where / you are, Jim.

Jim stays seated.

Brenda Jim, please.

Dad Brenda! She is my wife.

Mark (standing) Well, if Nana’s going to bed, I’m going upstairs an’ all –

Dad Mark, SIT DOWN! Everyone! Sit Your Fucking Arses DOWN! (Pause.) Brenda.

Brenda (hovering over Mam) Jim.

Dad Don’t Jim, just sit. Right?

Long pause. Brenda goes and sits.

Dad (pause; goes and sits) Meant to be bloody Christmas for Val, and all of you are –

The lights in the room return. The record player fuzzes, the needle on the vinyl.

Dad Oh, that were (’bout bloody) . . . (Sighs.) What’s that noise?

Jim (making to stand) Needle on t’ record player –

Dad Leave it! Just . . . sit . . .

Mam groans.

Dad ’S all right, Val, we’re here. Have another bite. (Tries.) You’re all right . . .

Silence as Dad eats. Jim watches Mam. The fuzzy sound of the needle tripping over the spinning vinyl. Suddenly, Jim stands with haste and goes to stop the record player. He stands facing it for a moment, back turned. He sits back at the table.

They eat. Jim catches Brenda’s eye. He looks at Mam. He looks at Mark. He fiddles around with his cutlery. . . before slowly standing.

Dad What you on wi’, Jim?

Jim is still at the table. Beat. He slowly walks over to some coat hooks and begins slipping on his jacket and boots. Dad stands.

Dad Oy, what you doing, where do you think you’re going?

Jim Going to work . . .

Dad Eh? Work? What work? There is no work! Sit back down.

Jim Going down yard.

Dad You what?

Jim See . . . see if there’s owt doing.

Dad Jim. Sit your arse down now! What’s brought this on?

Jim I’m sorry, Dad.

Dad Don’t be, on about ‘sorry’, just sit.

Jim Mam needs to be in the home, Dad.

Dad She’s in a . . . this – and, that in’t your decision, Jim. It’s mine! She’s, she’s my wife!! And she’s in a home, our home!

Jim I’m / sorry, Dad.

Dad Sit down!

Jim Mark. Go upstairs.

Dad Eh?

Jim Get your work done.

Mark (standing) Dad?

Dad Work, what work? Sit that bony little backside down now, Mark! Jim!

Mark Dad?

Jim Mark’s . . . got work to do, Dad. He’s studying. / He’s got exams.

Dad Studying? What? / What you on wi’?

Mark Dad don’t, you’re right, it in’t time.

Dad What about factory?

Jim There in’t one. And he in’t taking driving lessons, not yet. We just . . . didn’t want to tell you yet. / ’Cause of Mam. I’m sorry.

Mark I’m sorry, Granddad. / (Oh fuck.)

Dad I don’t . . . I can’t . . . Yer what? Just . . . look. Can we . . . why do you have go and, and, and ruin everything? This was meant to be for your mam, so can we just sit down, and then we’ll, we’ll sort it out. (Pause.) What you’re playing at? Sit. (Waits.) You aren’t man of this house, Jim, I am! And she, she is . . . (Struggling.) She is My Wife! You’ve no right. She’s My Wife! Now, SIT!

Jim doesn’t move. Dad grabs him by the back of the neck and pushes him down.

Brenda Jim!

Mark Granddad, don’t! Dad, I’m sorry!

Jim breaks free and stands back up. He can’t look Dad in the face. Dad hits him.

Brenda Terry, no!

Dad Sit down!

Jim tries to stay upright from the blow. Beat. Dad hits him again.

Dad SIT! DOWN!

Jim You’re . . . going wrong way. Dad. Me logbook’s out window. Flashing me lights . . .

Dad And what do you – what would you know, eh?! She ever tell yer? Say owt to you?

Jim No, Dad. That’s point. She never gorra chance. She’s . . . she’s going. . .

Dad What, let you in on all her little secrets, did she? Tell you stuff she never told me? On that fucking ‘day out’ of yourn?! You coming home, grinning.

Jim Dad . . .

Dad Where did you go, eh? What did you do that were so fucking great?! WHAT?!

Jim Just . . . (Sighs. Exhausted.) Blackpool, Dad. We just . . . fancied going across. To Blackpool. Just sat on the beach . . . went to the fair . . . bought an ice cream . . . which we shared . . .

Dad (looks at him) Please . . . sit down . . .

Jim (shakes his head) . . . I can’t . . .

Beat. Dad grabs him, hard, and shoves him over towards the dinner table. Mark and Brenda scream, trying to pull Dad off.

Brenda NO! / What are you doing?!

Mark / Granddad, let go of him! DAD!

Dad You’ll do as you’re bloody told! This is my house!

Brenda Terry! We’re only thinking / of you and Val!

Dad Leave off, Brenda! I’ve had enough!

Brenda / Jim.

Mark Granddad!

Dad She is my wife! / And it’s Christmas!

Brenda Let go of him,/ Terry. Please!

Mark It in’t even fucking Christmas!

He grabs hold of the wires and yanks the Christmas lights from the wall socket. There’s a spark. Brenda screams as the room descends into darkness.

Dad Awh, not again!

Brenda What were that spark?

Jim Mark?

Dad / These flaming lights.

Brenda Mark? Terry, it’s summat else! Where’s your torch?

Jim I’ll gerrit.

Brenda (screaming) Mark! He’s on the floor! Mark! / Can you hear me?

Dad Eh? What’s that?

Jim flicks on the torch. We see Mark lying on the floor. Brenda with him.

Brenda MARK! / Can you hear me, love!

Dad Bloody hell. It’s them flaming lights!

Brenda Don’t touch him, he’s had a shock. Jim, are the phones working?

Jim stumbles over to the phone. Brenda gets Mark into the recovery position.

Brenda (’S all right, Mark. / Just a little shock, we’ll get you sorted.)

Jim / Hello?

Dad Is he all right, Bren?

Jim Hello?! / (Phone down.) There’s no – it in’t working!

Mam groans.

Dad All right, Val, with / you in a sec.

Brenda (stroking his hair) Shh, all right Mark, OK.

Jim (opening the front door) Neighbours have lights on, must just be ours. I’ll run across, call an ambulance –

Brenda No, Jim, they’re not working, they’re all on bloody strike!

Jim Shit!

Jim runs outside. We hear him screaming for help. Mark starts having a fit, body shaking violently, vomiting from the mouth. Brenda tries to hold him.

Brenda Oh God. Mark?

Dad ’S up? / What’s he doing?

Brenda Mark? Can you hear me, love? It’s all right. It’s all right. I’ve got you . . . (Shh . . .)

Dad All that lovely dinner, Bren. All that lovely dinner . . .

Mark’s fit eases, and stops. Brenda holds him, stroking his hair. . .

Brenda (softly) Shh. ’S OK. You’re gonna be fine. Dad’s gone to get some help. Shh. Look at your hair, eh. All spikey, at last. Look at that. All punk rocker, like you’ve wanted.

Jim bursts back in, and goes to pick Mark up.

Jim Fuck it, look, I’ll just carry him, we’ll walk!

Brenda Jim, no, let’s / knock on other –

Jim It’s only ten minutes, I can do it.

Brenda He’s just been / fitting, Jim . . .

Dad All right, need a hand?

Jim (lifting him) Eeh dear, there we go, Mark. There we are. God, light as a feather, aren’t you . . . (Light as a feather . . .)

Dad What shall . . . (Struggling.) I’m awful s-sorry, Jim, I . . .

Jim Just ge’orr, Dad, will you?! I’ve had enough!

Dad I was . . . / only trying to . . .

Jim Look at what you’ve done, you daft old . . .

Brenda Jim.

Jim kicks his Dad in the leg while holding Mark. Dad stumbles a bit.

Dad I’m . . . sorry . . .

Jim THIS IS MY SON LYING HERE! IT’S MY SON . . .

He weeps. Dad watches. Brenda leads him away.

Brenda Come on . . . we’ve got to go. Just wait here, Terry, we’ll . . . Just wait here.

Brenda and Jim exit with Mark. Dad stands alone, holding the torch. From the darkness, Mam starts singing. Dad turns and slowly leads the torch light to her face.

Mam

‘It’s a lovely day tomorrow,

Tomorrow is a lovely day,

Come and feast your tear-dimmed eyes,

On tomorrow’s clear blue sky . . . ’





Scene Two


March 1979. The living room. Jim stands, dressed in a black suit, hands in his pocket. The door is open. Sound of a boot slamming. Brenda comes in and begins putting things in her purse. She applies some lipstick. Dad comes from upstairs.

Brenda All right, Terry?

Dad (fiddling with his cufflinks) Awh, I can’t do these . . . bloody . . .

Jim Here.

Brenda I’ve put your coat in the boot of the car, Terry.

Dad What’s that?

Brenda Your coat, it’s in the boot. Need to getting going, want to be at t’ funeral home before the others don’t we? Jim, do we park there and . . . ?

Jim Yeah, they uh . . . drive us to t’ cemetery. Then drop us back after.

Dad Need a glass of water. Anyone else want a glass of water? (Exiting into the kitchen.)

Brenda You OK?

Jim (pause, nods) You?

Brenda Yeah. OK.

They hug. Mark comes down the stairs, dressed in a suit.

Brenda All right, Mark? What’s matter with your hair?!

Mark I know, it won’t bloody go down now!

Jim You put some cream on it?

Mark Had it cut, cream on it, still won’t go flat! Tell you what, punk better stay in fashion for ever otherwise I’m fucked –

Jim Mark.

Mark Buggered.

Jim Thank you.

Dad (entering with a paper) He lost vote, then. Cor, general election, hell’s bells. Past few months, I doubt they’ll get in again. Which means the bloody Tories. Eh, and not just the bloody Tories but that bloody woman! Bah! Well, say goodbye to life as we know it, blimey . . .

Brenda Everybody ready? Mark, you got that reading?

Mark Uh, I’ve . . . I’ve added a bit. (Unfolding a piece of paper.) Just thought . . . something at end might be . . . It’s last few lines of that play, Dad. ’Member? Thought it might be appropriate, like . . . Dunno.

Jim (reading)

‘Now that civil wounds are stopped, peace lives here again.

That she may long live here, God say Amen.’

Dad Civil wounds stopped? Ah, well, we’ll wait and see.

Jim Ay, it’ll do.

Dad Course you know what wounds are healed wi’? Scabs. And you can’t rely on scabs.

Jim Da-ad.

Dad Oh, I’m only messing. Warning you, though, lad. Just wait and bloody see.

Brenda Right, that’s enough bloodys and fucks for today, OK? And enough frigging politics. Just have a day of, will you? Jim, close front door behind us. We’re off.

Brenda exits through the front door, followed by Mark. Jim waits for Dad, who is sorting things in his pockets and arranging his tie.

Dad Hate these bloody things, always have done. (Sighs.) Awh God. I’m dreading this. (Pause. Struggles a bit.) I . . . I don’t want to have a look at her in t’ coffin, Jim, it’s . . . Could you gerr’em to seal before I go in, like? I can’t bear to see her like that . . .

Jim Yeah . . .

Dad I want to remember that girl. What I looked down at from the balcony. Of Beverley Road Baths. Folk keep going on about today being so as you can say goodbye. I said bye long time ago. Them last months. That weren’t her. She . . . well . . . this is just . . . final whistle, in’t it? (With his tie.) How’s it looking?

Dad stands in front of Jim, who wiggles his tie a bit, gently dusting down his arms.

Dad ’Member to close the door to, behind yer.

Dad leaves. Jim stands alone. Hands in his pocket. Looking at Mam’s chair.

Lights down.





A History of Falling Things





Cast List


A History of Falling Things was first performed at Clwyd Theatr Cymru, Mold, on 23 April 2009. The cast was as follows:

Robin

Sion Pritchard



Jacqui

Katie McGuinness



Jimmy

Francois Pandolfo

Di Botcher

Philip Bretherton



Boy

Matthew Cavallini, Adam Walker



Director Kate Wasserberg

Designer Mark Bailey

Lighting Tom White

Sound Kevin Heyes

Animator Paddy Molloy

Choreographer Francesca Jaynes





Characters


Robin

Jacqui

Lesley, Robin’s mum

Reece, Jacqui’s dad

Jimmy, a courier

Boy

John, Robin’s psychologist (voice only)





A Note on the Text


A forward slash ( / ) indicates that the next speaker overlaps at this point.

(Speech in brackets) indicates that the line is spoken internally/ sotto voce.

Speech that is indented thus indicates that a character is addressing the audience directly.





A Boy stands, waiting. He wears kneepads, a cycling helmet, and a rucksack.

A shoe falls from the sky and hits the Boy on his head. The Boy looks around and sees the shoe on the floor. He picks it up. He looks up at the sky, then back at the shoe. He looks up at the sky, then back at the shoe. He looks up at the sky. . .

He puts the shoe in his bag and leaves. . .



A kitchen table. The Boy goes and sits under it, replacing his cycling helmet with a saucepan. From his rucksack he retrieves some books.

The Boy looks out from under the table, up towards the sky. He takes out a Walkman and puts on a pair of headphones, pressing ‘play’.

Music.

The Boy opens a book and begins to read, all the time taking intervals to look out from the table, up towards the sky.

Fade to black, as the music grows. . .



Lights up on the kitchen table. Robin is sitting underneath it, scribbling in a notebook. A laptop nearby. As the music fades, he looks up to the sky, then carries on writing. He looks over what he’s done.

Robin (reading) ‘“Will you play . . . ” ’

Scribbles something out. Rewrites.

‘“Can I play with you?” Pluto said.

As Mars and Venus shook their head.

“But why not?” Pluto did say’ –

No, that’s crap. (Say, may, way . . . play?) Play?

(Writing.) ‘Pluto was . . .

‘Pluto was sad. “Why can’t I play?”

You let me join in yesterday.”’ (Yes.)

‘“You aren’t one of us,” hissed Saturn

As the rest began their circling pattern,

And no matter how much Pluto tried,

He just had to watch. Alone. Outsi – ’

A beep from the computer. Robin leans over and checks it.

Robin Oh. Hello. Where did you come from?

Blackout.



The voices of Robin and John, talking over the phone.

John (off) Where are you now?

Robin (off) What do you mean?

John (off) You know what I mean, Robin. Where are you speaking to me from? Are you under the table again?

Robin (off) I . . . (Sighs.) I’m moving around. I’m –

John (off) All right. All right. So, what’s new? How are you doing?

Robin (off) Nothing’s . . . I’ve – there’s – I’m never any different. John. Nothing changes. Since yesterday, since the day before, since . . . well, since then, really.

John (off) But now you’re a man.

Robin (off) But hardly anything has changed. / That’s my –

John (off) And whose fault is that?

Robin (off) What?

John (off) And whose fault is that, Robin?

Robin (off) I’m not finding this particularly helpful today.

John (off) Because you aren’t trying. And you’re not paying for it, she is.

Robin (off) Pay . . . (Sighs.) Paying for me to get worse. / (Waste of money.)

John (off) You aren’t getting worse. What was that?

Robin (off) I’m . . . I’m staying – it – that, that’s what I’m – it, it, it –

John (off) Calm down, take a breath.

Robin (off) It – I wasn’t –

John (off) Calm down.

Robin (off) I wasn’t stuttering, I was, I was emphasising. Just –

John (off) Just calm down.

Robin (off) I’m staying the same, is what I’m saying. So this, this can’t be helping –

John (off) You’re not getting worse, though, are you? Maybe if we didn’t speak, if we didn’t have these little chats, you would be getting worse. Ipso facto, you’re getting better.

Robin (off) (Ipso facto.)

John (off) One minute, Robin, and then I have to go.

Robin (off) I need to tell you something.

John (off) What?

Robin (off) Before you hang up. Um. (Urgh.) OK.

John (off) Go on.

Robin (off) I’d like her to hear about it, but not – I don’t want her to hear about it in a way that makes it sound like I’ve said it to you so that you’ll say it to her, because even though I am, that will make it sound bigger than it is, when it –

John (off) Robin, please.

Robin (off) I’ve met someone.

John (off) You’ve met someone?

Robin (off) I’ve. Met someone.

John (off) You. You’ve Met Someone, I don’t under – How? Are we talking about a girl? How?

Robin (off) Just . . . feeling around. In the dark. Trying to find something. My world, it’s so . . . and in the middle of the darkness. After lots – after scrabbling around. I found her.

John (off) Sorry, wait, she’s . . . How did you find her?

Robin (off) I just . . . (Laughing.) I just typed it in.

John (off) Typed it in?

Robin (off) Into one of the things, the search engines, just typed it in.

On a screen, a flashing curser types out the word as he spells it.

Robin (off) K-E-R-A-U-N-O-T-H-N-E-T-O-P-H-O-B-I-A. Keraunothnetophobia.

‘A fear of falling satellites’.

The screen disappears. Darkness again.

Just like that. And there’s this chat – this chatroom type thing and everyone – from all over the world, everyone just . . . some nutters at first. But then. This girl. Right here, who lives right . . . and so I just . . . I just typed –

He can be heard onstage, which is still in darkness. Typing.

Robin Hello.

Robin (off – back on the phone) And so she typed –

Jacqui can be heard on stage, still in darkness. Typing.

Jacqui Hello.

Robin Hello. (Laughing.) Hi.

Jacqui Hi. How are you?

Robin I’m OK. Thank you. How are you?

Jacqui I’m OK. Would you like to see me?

Robin What do you . . . how do / you mean?

Jacqui See each other, I mean, sorry. Do you have a webcam?

Robin (off, on the phone) And so, bing. Light.

Jacqui appears in a spot, stage left. Robin can still only be heard.

Jacqui Hello?

Robin Hello. Wow. (Sorry.) I (shit) I –

Jacqui Can you see me? You’re not on.

Robin What?

Jacqui I can’t see you.

Robin Oh. I can see you.

Jacqui Something’s wrong. Is it installed?

Robin Let me . . . (piece of) . . . Sorry. One second.

Jacqui (waiting) Feels weird, being looked at when . . . when I can’t see –

Robin There.

Robin appears in a light, stage right. They look at each other.

Robin How’s that?

Jacqui Oh. Yes. There you are. (Beat. Smiles.) Hello.

Robin Hello. (Laughing.) Sorry. I’ve . . . I’ve never done this before.

Jacqui Oh God, no. No, me neither. Not really.

Robin So, is your name really CityKitten84 or . . . ?

Jacqui Huh. Um. It’s Jacqui.

Robin Robin. Hello.

Jacqui Hi. (Pause.) You’re how I imagined actually.

Robin Really?

Jacqui From what you’ve – the things you written to . . . in the chatroom. Thing. Yeah. A bit. (Giggles.) I’m embarrassed, sorry.

Robin You look . . .

Jacqui What?

Robin (pause) Really . . .

Jacqui (beat; giggles) What?

Robin (beat; shrugs) Perfect.

Jacqui (speaking out) He didn’t say that.

Robin Yes I did.

Jacqui No.

Robin In my head that’s what I said.

Jacqui But out of your mouth came . . . ?

Robin It had been a while. I was out of practice.

Jacqui He said I looked ‘good’. ‘In the face.’

Robin That is what I said. But you found it funny.

Jacqui I found it charming. In a bumbling kind of way. (Laughing.) ‘You look good in the face.’

Robin Thank you.

Jacqui No. Thank you.

Jacqui So that’s your place. Let me see.

As Robin turns and looks around, light spreads across his living room, stage right.

What we see is half a room, cut off in the middle. At the cut-off point, the furniture there too is cut in half. Half a bookcase, half a sofa, a door at the rear, a desk at the back, etc. It’s evening. . .

Robin Just your average living room in your average house / in your –

Jacqui Detached, semi-detached, flat?

Robin House. Terraced.

Jacqui Ha! Mine too. Is it yours?

Robin Yes. What do you mean?

Jacqui Do you own it?

Robin Oh no. No, God, no way. I rent it.

Jacqui Right. Course.

Robin But I rent it On My Own. That’s what I thought you –

Jacqui Yeah, no. It’s very nice.

Robin Even round here, now, prices have . . . even now, it –

Jacqui Lots of books.

Robin Yeah, lots of books. I – that’s what I – I’m a writer.

Jacqui A writer? Really?

Robin That’s how I . . . you know, survive.

Jacqui What do you write?

Robin You know. Books. Things. Children’s books.

Jacqui Ha! That’s really cool.

Robin Well. And yours? You, yours?

Jacqui Oh. Just . . .

She turns and looks around, as light spreads round her bedroom, also cut in half, so that, apart from the difference in appearance, they could perhaps be two halves of the same room, with a ‘no man’s land’ of darkness centre stage, between them.

Jacqui’s bedroom looks like that of a young girl. Single bed. Bright colours. . .

Robin Wow. God, so that’s your –

Jacqui I know what you’re thinking.

Robin I’m not / thinking anything.

Jacqui It’s my old room. I’ve had it since I was, like, six. So . . .

Robin Was it weird. Coming back?

Jacqui It was depressing. And yet a little bit . . . I don’t know. A little bit not.

Robin Different from London.

Jacqui Uh, yeah. Ah-uh. Yeah, different from London.

Robin But ‘home’, though.

Jacqui Yeah. Nice to have my dad close again. In some ways.

Robin How close?

Jacqui His bedroom is next door.

Robin That must be weird.

Jacqui Yeah, a little. Everyone is trying hard to understand. It’s not easy for them. Particularly Dad.

Robin It’s not easy for anyone.

Jacqui He says I’ve lost my accent.

Robin Yeah, my folks said that. When I came back from uni. You get it back, though.

Jacqui You went to uni?

Robin Yeah. Well, kinda. For a bit.

Jacqui It just sounded like . . . from the things you posted on the . . . on the thing. It just sounded like you’d been like this. For a while.

Robin I didn’t finish. It got too . . . um, ‘much’. But I’ve done – I did one of those, uh, Open University thingies instead. From here. From home.

Jacqui Oh, right. OK. Well done.

Robin I’m glad you moved back. It’s nice to feel like someone is close. For what it’s worth.

Jacqui Yeah. Well. For what it’s worth.

Robin Right. What?

Jacqui Well, you may as well be in Dubai or, or, you know –

Robin Sure.

Jacqui But no, you’re right, it’s nice. For what it’s worth. (Beat. Referencing her clothes.) I wish I’d put something nicer on. This –

Robin Don’t be – you look so. Nice.

Jacqui Well. Thank you. I . . . (Looks down at herself.)

Jacqui (speaking out) I knew what he was thinking.

Robin I wasn’t.

Jacqui You were. You thought I’d planned it, got done up to look good for you.

Robin Well I, it had crossed my mind. / But I liked that.

Jacqui Well, I didn’t. When I’m working, even now it’s from home, if I am working, it helps, it helps me to feel smart. Like I’m out there. It wasn’t for you.

Robin OK, OK.

Robin disappears. Jacqui faces out and mouths ‘It was’ to us. Robin reappears.

Jacqui So I’ll see you again? Maybe tomorrow?

Robin Yeah. Tomorrow’s good.

Blackout.



Robin and John over the phone again.

John (off) Have the dreams changed since you met? What fell in your dream last night?

Robin (off) Rain, at first. Like always. And then frogs.

John (off) Oh, the frogs are back, are they? I thought we’d got rid of the frogs.

Robin (off) No. They’re back. And then it was pennies.

John (off) Pennies from heaven, eh? And then the usual.

Robin (off) There was something else. Before the usual.

John (off) Before the satellite? What?

Robin (off) . . . Blood.

John (off) Blood?

Robin (off) Yes.

John (off) That’s a new one.

Robin (off) What do you think it means?

John (pause, off) Probably nothing.



Robin and Jacqui on their respective sides.

Jacqui What started you writing for children?

Robin I just. (Shrugs.) Wanted to tell stories. Loved them growing up. Even though they generally scared the shit out of me. Think children’s stories can be the scariest of all. Watership Down, do you remember that? Rabbits being eaten by dogs or shot or run over by cars; that was nice.

Jacqui I was big into Disney. Used to dress up, had a little crown and everything. My mum bought me this music box. Spinning princess in it. Still have it. (Loser.) She used to sing this song to me and then she found this box, in a shop somewhere, that was playing the same thing. And she couldn’t believe it. So she bought it for me to have when she wasn’t here.

Which was more and more, as it goes.

Robin (beat) Chicken Licken, course. The chicken who believed the sky was falling down.

Jacqui Yeah, course. Even though it wasn’t.

Robin Even though it wasn’t. Robin Hood.

Jacqui Your namesake.

Robin Exactly. Used to pretend. Save Maid Marian. From the castle.

Jacqui There aren’t any Jacquis in fairytales. Sucks. No one cool was ever called Jacqui.

Robin Jackie Kennedy.

Jacqui Oh yeah. S’pose.

Robin The Snowman. See, that was scary.

Jacqui That wasn’t scary.

Robin Uh, the Snowman melts to death at the end.

Jacqui Oh yeah.

Robin I’m telling you. Kids’ stories.

Jacqui Well, I guess it’s about helping them learn.

Robin Learn what?

Jacqui That life is full of danger. And sadness. And disappointment.

Robin Well, they certainly gave me nightmares.

Jacqui I get nightmares. My whole . . . my thing started with . . . you know . . .

Robin Did it?

Jacqui It’s getting late again. I should probably –

Robin Yeah. Course.

Jacqui (beat) Sweet dreams.

Jacqui disappears. The middle area between the two rooms opens out, as Robin enters the space, holding an umbrella. It’s raining. Music.

Robin Cats and dogs. From what I can tell it has never ever rained cats and dogs anywhere. What has been recorded though is frogs . . .

It starts to rain frogs.

Lots and lots of frogs. Above Minneapolis in 1901, a green cloud appeared overhead, and a deluge began. While driving through Scotland in 1955, falling frogs pelted Nellie Straw’s car. They fell again as recently as 1981 in Nephalion, Greece, where frogs, at the time, were not even indigenous. They are now. The only hypothesis the scientists can cling to is the waterspout phenomenon, where animals are sucked up into the sky and carried through clouds before being dropped over land. Then there’s the fish.

It starts to rain fish.

Some perch fish showered a parking lot in Northern Australia, some four hundred miles from the sea. Catfish, bream and bass rained down over Alabama in 1956. Local legend records that they were very, very tasty.

It starts to rain notes, and then coins.

Lucky were a French community in 1957 when thousand-franc notes floated down over their village from a clear blue sky. Less lucky, depending on how you look at it, were the party of schoolchildren being walked home in Manchester, 1982, when they began to be pelted with pennies. The more resourceful ones not injured gathered as much as they could and ran straight to the nearest sweet shop. Stranger still, the coins that fell over Gorky in Russia were from the sixteenth century and could not be accounted for.

Various objects now begin to rain down.

The house in Evans, Colorado, that for the past one hundred and twenty odd years has suffered the occasional shower of corn, battering its roof. Then there is the as yet unproven myth of the Japanese fishing boat sunk off the coast of Siberia – the traumatised crew insisting wholeheartedly that the hole in the middle of the deck was caused by nothing other than . . . a cow. Falling from above.

It begins to rain blood.

And in Li Yan, France, and in Calabria, Italy, and in California, and in India, where recorded, falling from the sky, were tiny little droplets of scarlet-red blood . . .

Electronic beeps. Robin looks up as a shadow is cast over him. The sound of a heavy object plummeting to the ground. Robin ducks. The noise is cut off instantly by:



Jacqui and Robin on their respective sides. Drinking tea.

Robin You’ve never seen it.

Jacqui Is it that one with the aliens?

Robin No, that’s Close Encounter. Brief Encounter is the black-and-white one. With the train station? We could watch it together. Well, not together, but . . . you know. At the same time.

Jacqui Yeah, OK. Like a date.

Robin Uh. Yeah. I . . . Do, do British people, do they go on dates? I always thought that was American.

Jacqui I went on dates. In London. Kind of.

Robin Did you?

Jacqui I like the way that women used to be kissed in the old films. The way they’d get flung around. By Clark Gable or someone, and they’d . . . (Trying to demonstrate.) Sort of, they’d get the girl’s, this hair, kind of get flicked round and lean on his hands . . .

Robin I used to love how men held guns. Be like this. (Demonstrates.) Elbows pointing straight out from the hip. Like that. Keeping it close. Now in films it’s up and out.

Stands and holds his arm out, parallel with his shoulder, finger pointed.

Like, and legs spread apart. More showy, I suppose.

Jacqui My dad says in classic love films, the colour ones are more likely to see them get together in the end and the black-and-white ones, they’re more likely to end up apart.

Robin Dunno, maybe. They end up apart in Casablanca. And Brief Encounter.

Jacqui Well, that’s why life should be in full colour then.

Robin Speaking of, of, um . . . stories, I’ve got you – I’ve got a surprise for you. A present. Well it’s just a book. Well it, my, it’s just my book. Actually.

Jacqui Yours? Oh my God.

Robin I mean, don’t . . . You know, I mean I get copies free so don’t . . . it’s no biggie. / But.

Jacqui Oh, don’t ruin it. That’s really kind. Thank you. But I can just order one online.

Robin No, I’ve got a courier. Coming. To, to pick it up, and bring it round.

Jacqui Robin.

Robin Don’t think anything of it.

Knock at Robin’s door. Robin opens it. Jimmy, the courier, stands there.

Jimmy Pick up from Robin?

Robin Hi. Yes, here. Uh, address is, is on the front.

Jimmy Cheers.

A space opens out between the rooms: the doorway to Jacqui’s house.

Reece opens the door to Jimmy.

Reece Oh. Hello.

Jimmy Delivery.

Reece I didn’t order a pizza.

Jimmy It’s not a pizza, it’s a package.

Reece A package, who for?

Jimmy For Jacqui, says here.

Reece Oh. Right. She’s – that’s my daughter, she’s upstairs.

Jimmy Sign here, please.

Reece She’s always getting packages from London, you know. Bit late for work, though.

Jimmy I don’t think it was work. It comes from a boy.

Reece A boy?

Jimmy Well, a young man I suppose. Charlton Street, five minutes or so.

Reece From a boy on Charlton Street? She doesn’t know any boys round here, Jacqui; she doesn’t know anyone any more.

Jimmy Right.

Reece Locks herself away in her room, you see.

Jimmy Right. Have a good night then, sir.

Reece Oh yes. Right-oh. Good night.



Jacqui’s room. Reece enters.

Jacqui Dad.

Reece Package here.

Jacqui Thank you.

Reece From a boy, he said, the fella.

Jacqui Uh, yeah. A friend. I’ve met. Sort of.

Reece A friend you’ve met sort of?

Jacqui Yes.

Reece (at the computer) What, on that thing?

Jacqui Yes.

Reece Oh. Oh right.

Jacqui Is he still here? The courier?

Reece He’s just getting on his bike. (Laughs.) ‘On yer bike’, eh. Ha ha. Do you want me to bring your tea up or are you coming down?

Jacqui Dad, can you do me a favour? (With a card.) Can you ask him to deliver this?

Reece What’s that now?

Jacqui Please, here’s some money.

Reece Well, he’s going, he’s probably got another job –

Jacqui No, look, he’s still outside on his radio.

Reece Oh Lord, all – (Sighs, takes the card.) Wait here.



Downstairs. Reece with Jimmy at the doorway.

Reece I am sorry.

Jimmy It’s just I only had one more and then was clocking off, you see?

Reece I’ve got some money.

Jimmy It’s not the money, I’m meant to be going out.

Reece Oh, anywhere nice?

Jimmy Just a curry. With the missus. Well, I say missus, we haven’t really had that chat yet, but –

Reece I would really appreciate it. It seems to mean a lot to her, for some reason.

Jimmy (counting the money) I’m not sure this is enough, to be honest with you.

Reece (taking out his wallet and more cash) Oh, I see. Uh, there you go then.

Jimmy Oh. Thank you. Well, I’ll see what I can do.

Reece You know, I reckon that’s the problem with young people in cities, don’t you? Because they can get anything they want instantly they have no sense of prudence. Or of waiting for anything. She could have just as well stuck a stamp on that and I’d have posted it and it could’ve been there the day after next, but oh no. Must have now.

Jimmy I know what you mean, sir, I know exactly what you mean.

Reece (speaking out) We laugh about this little exchange in months to come.

Jimmy We do, he’s right.

Jimmy Good evening then, sir.

Reece Ay, go well, young man, go well.



Knock on Robin’s door. Robin answers.

Robin Oh. Hello again. Was there a problem?

Jimmy Pick up and return. (Handing him the card.) Here. For you.

Robin From her?

Jimmy Yes, from her. Via him, from her.

Robin Via him?

Jimmy Her dad. Nice chap. I’ll see you later.

Robin Wait. Could you –?

Jimmy Oh no, no, that’s it, I’m off home –

Robin No, just a question, I . . . it’s just . . . I, I wanted to know. What she was. Like.

Jimmy Who? The girl?

Robin Yeah.

Jimmy You’ve never met?

Robin No.

Jimmy I didn’t see her. She was upstairs. It was her dad who I dealt with, I’m afraid.

Robin Oh. OK.

Jimmy She seemed nice from downstairs, though.

Robin Really, you can tell?

Jimmy Sometimes you just get a vibe, don’t you?

Robin Yeah. I suppose you do.

Jimmy leaves. Robin closes the door. Begins opening the card. Turns to Jacqui.

Robin What’s this?

Jacqui I just wanted . . . I don’t know. I just wanted you to have something there that . . . I don’t know, that used to be here. Some . . . (Sighs.) contact, or (I don’t know.)

Robin (reads the front) ‘Congratulations on passing your driving test.’ Awh.

Jacqui Sorry. Only one I had. For my nephew.

Robin Oh, I’m sorry, you shouldn’t have –

Jacqui No that’s OK, he won’t pass. Least I bloody hope not.

Robin ‘Thanks for the book. Love, Jacqui.’ (Pause.) Thank you.

Jacqui No. (With her book.) No, thank you.

Robin (Lovely handwriting.)

Jacqui Jesus, you must be so proud. The illustrations are beautiful. Did you do them?

Robin No.

Jacqui Well. I can’t wait to read it. ‘Ages five and above.’ Good, I’m covered.

Robin (sniffing the card) Your hand cream.

Jacqui You didn’t do that.

Robin No, I know. I waited till later. When I was on my own. Thought you might have found it pervy.

Jacqui And I would have done. But also sweet.

Robin ‘U-I.’

Jacqui Uh?

Robin Jacqui – ‘U-I’ at the end. In my head it, it’s always been ‘I-E’. (Strange.)

Jacqui Nope, just ‘U’ and ‘I’. Quite boring, actually. Hate it.

Robin Oh I don’t know, I . . . I quite like it. That it’s just. ‘U’ and ‘I’.

They stand smiling at each other for a while. Blackout on Jacqui and her house.



Lesley enters Robin’s house carrying bags of food and other more random objects, such as framed photos, vases and plastic flowers, cleaning products etc. She places items, retrieves others and cleans throughout, never stopping.

Lesley Phoah, cold outside today, Gordon Bennett, cold for May, anyway. Well, compared to last year, remember the spring we had last year, absolutely boiling, wasn’t it? Too hot, I thought, but then again, think of the summer, well, whatever summer we had, which wasn’t much, remember? Not that it makes much difference to you in here. Still, with the windows open and everything. So maybe that’s it, maybe rotten spring means we’ll actually have a bloody summer, who knows? No rhyme or reason most of the time, is there, the weather? Don’t suppose it was quite the same down there, was it, when you were at what’s-it. Even on a little island like this, they say there’s differences, don’t they? Well, you only have to look at Scotland, their weather, like another country isn’t it – well, I suppose it is another country, in many ways, well, in most ways really. That accent, Christ. Got you a bit of the stew we had last night, here.

Robin (takes it) Thank you.

Lesley Me and your brother, he was round with the kids, don’t know where she was, got the impression I wasn’t meant to ask, if you know what I mean, look on the kids’ face though, patently obvious – is that right, patently? blatantly? – anyhow, ‘apparent’, it was, they’d been rowing. Hope they sort it out. Your father and I never rowed. Above and beyond what you’d expect in a marriage, course. Nowadays, why is it nowadays you make it all so complicated? I reckon it’s cos you all watch too many of them soaps.

Robin I don’t even ask any more. What she brings round in them bags, I don’t care. I’ll wake up and there’ll be a new family photo on my bedside table, staring at me, but I just can’t ask any more. And it makes absolutely no odds to her whether I notice or not.

Lesley Had a go at the path thing today? Outside, like John said? Couple of steps? One more each day? Reckon that’s a good idea, I do.

Robin It doesn’t work, Mum.

Lesley You should listen to John more, he’s a very talented man.

Robin He’s a waste of your money, Mum.

Lesley Right, there we are, jobs done, I’ll have to get back, that phone’ll be ringing off the hook and they’ll be wondering where I’ve got to. Anything you want to tell me? Any news? That you’ve not brought up yet? Work stuff, or . . . social? Anything?

Robin Uh, well . . . I, I do meet people, you know, Mum, I, I have. Met someone. Sort of.

Lesley A girl? (Laughs excitedly, covers her mouth.) Sorry. A girl?

Robin It’s no big deal, Mum, don’t / get worked –

Lesley Does she come round here, then?

Robin No.

Lesley No?

Robin No, she . . . we met, we’ve only met online.

Lesley Online?

Robin Yes.

Lesley On that thing?

Robin Yes.

Lesley (beat) Well that’s no good, is it?

Robin It’s fine.

Lesley You don’t know who it could be, could be some pervert, some fat old man.

Robin I’ve seen her.

Lesley You’ve seen her?

Robin We use a webcam.

Lesley You use a webcam?

Robin Mum, you’re doing it again!

Lesley No, love, that’s not right, you need more than that. It’s not enough.

Robin It’s no big deal.

Lesley You’ll see. In the end, it’s not enough. You’re a human, Robin, humans need physical contact. Something you are seriously malnutritioned in – is that right? Malnourished?

Robin I’m fine, Mum.

Lesley More than that, you’re a man, a growing man. And you’ve got urges. I know.

Robin (Oh God.)

Lesley And she’ll have ’em, as well. And people may say it isn’t important, but it is. You’ve got to be compatible. Sexually, I mean. And until / you try that, there’s –

Robin Oh God, aah! (Putting his fingers in his ears.) Ah-la-la-ah-yaaah! Mum, Christ!

Lesley We-ell. You know I’m right. I may be your mother but I’m also a woman.

Robin I’m, I’m either going to have to hit my head against the wall until I pass out or you’re going to have to stop talking. Please, please.

Lesley All right. You’ll find out before long. Half an hour, that stew, see you tomorrow.

She kisses him on the cheek and leaves.



Robin in his house, Jacqui in her room, each tying a balloon.

Jacqui (with a marker pen) So what am I doing . . . ?

Robin (with his own) You write my name. And I write yours.

Jacqui (laughing) This is ridiculous.

Robin I’m telling you. Read this story once, about a girl, young girl in Scotland. Wrote her name on a balloon, let it go from her back garden. Weeks later, girl with the same name, somewhere in England, standing in her garden, caught it as it floated down, and turned it round to read her own name. If we will it hard enough, it’ll reach me, and mine yours. Humour me. ‘Any way we can’, remember. Where you letting it go?

Jacqui Just out the window. You?

Robin Skylight, upstairs landing. (Shivers.) Ugh. Normally have to duck and run under that thing, but on this occasion, I’ll make a, a . . . you know. So. Ready?

They lean into the area, centre stage, and let go of the balloons. They float up.

Music, as they watch.

Fade out.



Day. Robin in his house. Jacqui is in her room.

Jacqui (referencing his books) Have you read them all?

Robin I’ve. I’ve read something ‘within’ them all. A chapter. Or whatever.

Jacqui What are they on? Mainly.

Robin Just everything. Random things. Got car manuals up there. Gardening. Walks in Nepal. Just stuff.

Jacqui Why?

Robin (shrugs) It’s how you learn, isn’t it?

Jacqui That a French one? You speak French?

Robin Uh, oui. Un peu.

Jacqui You ever been?

Robin No. (Beat. Confused.) No, of course not.

Pause.

Jacqui I couldn’t do this; just take a break like this normally. I need to feel like I’m . . . I’m still doing something, contributing. Can’t get used to working from home. Maybe it’s cos I’m in my old room. Bit different from Canary Wharf.

What do you miss the most? About out there. What is it that drives you crazy?

Robin (beat; shrugs) Nothing.

Jacqui Nothing; are you mad? I miss loads. I miss everything. I miss the city. Miss the buzz. The busyness. I miss . . . friends. I miss, I even miss having . . . (Sighs.) enemies. People to get one over on. Miss bars, clubs. Dancing. Dancing in a club. I miss catching someone’s eye. In passing. On a train, in the street. Just sharing something. Silently. With someone you don’t even know. (Pause.) I will get better. I will.

Robin (beat) What were the dreams?

Jacqui Uh?

Robin You said it started with dreams. Don’t have to tell me. Just . . .

Pause.

Jacqui Erm, well. Just, you know . . . just bad. Bad dreams. It didn’t actually begin with . . . satellites. It was . . . other things. At first.

Robin Me too.

Jacqui Started about three, four years – what date is it? Yeah, about four years ago. A very, erm . . . well, a very specific moment. Shall we say. I was on the Tube.

Robin The Tube? Surely one of the safest places you could be. From falling things.

Jacqui Yeah. You would think. I lived in Balham, when I was in London.

Robin ‘The gateway to the south.’ Huh.

Jacqui Yeah. What?

Robin Oh, just . . . Peter – it’s a Peter Sellers sketch.

Jacqui Right.

Robin (as Peter Sellers) ‘Ah, Bal-ham. The Gateway to the South.’

Jacqui Right.

Robin Sorry.

Jacqui There’s this plaque. In the ticket hall. About the people that died after a bomb dropped on the high street during the blitz.

Robin I’ve seen pictures of that. Of the crater and the bus –

Jacqui Well anyway. That’s where people would gather to be safe. Underground. But a bomb landed on the street. And rubble collapsed on to the station and shops and things fell into the crater and water pipes burst and people were crushed and buried and they died and I would often think about that – not too much – but sometimes, when I would travel to work in the morning. Thinking about what it would be like.

And then there was this morning one July.

A space opens out in the area between the rooms. A Tube seat.

Jacqui sits in it. Sounds of the Tube travelling – it suddenly grinds to a stop.

Jacqui I’d changed at Embankment and got on to the Circle Line. Never normally get a seat, that time of the day, Circle Line. But someone got off and I was the nearest so . . . and we were heading round to Liverpool Street. And just before we got to Aldgate, the train pulled to a halt. Nothing strange about that, always stopping. Stop, start, stop, start. Only it didn’t move for, like, ten minutes. And people were grumbling and sighing. And then the lights went out.

The lights flicker, and then die. A distant howling wind seems to build as though blowing down the cavernous tunnel. . .

And at first there was that titter of laughter, like ‘What next?’ And then more moaning. And then eventually there was just silence. And in the dark I started getting these flashes. Like flashes of being trapped. And the tunnels collapsing in. And rubble dropping down. And it felt like the oxygen was getting less and I felt like I was getting cramped and I started to begin panicking and then –

Sound of the engines starting up, as the light flickers back on and the train moves.

– we started reversing backwards. It was when we got out that we heard a bomb had gone off. Near Aldgate East. And three more around London . . .

She leaves the ‘Tube’ and returns to her room.

Everyone else . . . there was this sense of, like, ‘defiance’. Or . . . of just not being afraid. And a unity in ‘not being afraid’, but the problem was I was afraid. Of something completely different, which made me feel different to everyone else. And the dreams at first were of ceilings and roofs, and then of . . . planes . . . and then of . . . well, you know what. And who knows why? Maybe because everything else felt irrational. Freak occurrences. And I know satellites must seem, to normal people, must seem irrational. Maybe I just needed something that was always up there. That could come down at any point. Just to justify the way I was feeling –

Robin Nothing irrational about it. There are over two and a half thousand satellites up there, and that’s just the ones that are operating, it’s like a graveyard, a car park, like in February this year, when those Russian and American ones crashed into each other –

Jacqui Thanks, Robin.

Robin Oh. Sorry.

Jacqui I had to take sabbatical. Felt like a fraud. There were some people in my office who’d lost friends over there, in New York, in our office over there when . . . and I didn’t, hadn’t lost anyone, then or now, but . . . I couldn’t help it. Began to work from my flat more, hated going out. My friends, who had been supportive at first, began to . . . you know, ignore me. A bit. I wasn’t great to be around, end of the day.

Robin Yeah. Yeah, a lot of people found me . . . uh, irritating. When I had . . . ‘people’ . . .

Jacqui Eventually they, they agreed I could pretty much do most of it from here. If I wanted to move back up. Be around family. Full time. And over time I just . . . (Shrugs.) stopped leaving altogether . . .

But I’m gonna beat it. I am going to get better. So . . .

But I mean you, you’ve been . . . well. Like this. For ever.

Robin Well. In degrees. Since I was a little boy. I mean, I’d always had the need to feel . . . I dunno, enclosed, I suppose. Couldn’t poke a toe out from my duvet at night, case something came along and . . . or . . .

And then the time, erm . . . uh, the time when I was seven. And I was standing in the playground. Waiting to take my cycling proficiency test. And. And something hit me on the head. It was a shoe. A man’s shoe. And it came straight down. Not across. It didn’t have, like, an arc, like it had been thrown. It had fallen straight down. I picked it up. And I looked around; no one was looking, no one had seen. There was no laughing, no giggling. And on everyone’s feet were two shoes. And it was a man’s shoe anyway.

Jacqui Do you still have it?

Robin A black . . . a smart, black, size-eleven shoe. I looked up to see if there were any planes overhead, couldn’t see any. I couldn’t see a damn thing.

Pause.

Jacqui Did you pass your cycling proficiency?

Robin No. No, I turned round. Left the playground. And walked home.

It’s my fault. I’m told. By the people my mum sends to insult me. I indulged in the fear, I willed it to become all-consuming, apparently. I’m the kind of character that needs something such as this to patch together an identity. So there you go.

Jacqui It has been a long time.

Robin Look who’s talking.

Jacqui I’m not . . . I’m saying for me it’s only been a matter of years. I mean, when was the last time you went out?

Robin Uh, it didn’t go very well. I’ll say that much. And, and proved how I’m not ready. Despite everyone –

Jacqui What happened? You don’t have to say.

Robin It was. When my dad died. And it was his funeral. And I got to the end of the path, through the gate. Uh. Under the big tree by my road. It was pissing it down.

He enters the centre space. Under a tree. Rain and thunder. He holds himself tight.

Everyone was waiting in the cars. Mum. My brother, Mark. They, they’d tried to make it as easy as possible for me. The whole procession, pulling right up to my house. All I had to do was run across the road and get in. But then once I’m in the car, what then? Start screaming, have a panic attack? Mark and Mum had got sick of tugging on me, getting wet. Now they’re just sat in the front car. Faces appearing intermittently between the sweeps of the windscreen wipers. Imploring me just to come that little bit further. But I can’t. Looking at the hearse. ‘Dad’, written in flowers. I want to so much. So much. But I can’t. My brother’s face. I was ruining everything. But I’d gone as far as I could. So I turned around, and walked back inside.

He re-enters his house. Sits. Silence.

When they came back, my mum went next door to say thank you for something or other. I asked Mark how it had gone, and he said ‘Fine’. And then he hit me in the stomach. And it hurt. And I deserved it. But then as I was trying to stand he hit me again. And I didn’t think I deserved that. I was the one who had missed the funeral and I’d missed it because it was becoming about me and I didn’t want it to be about me, it was meant to be about Dad, and I couldn’t understand why he couldn’t understand that. I had wanted to go and stand up and talk about him. About the way he would do some proper old-school whistling as he mowed the lawn and the way he would drum with a fork on the biscuit tin; I’d wanted to do that. Mark went on about funerals being for the living, not the dead. And that day had been for Mum. And I had let her down. We haven’t really spoken since; not properly. And I don’t think that’s fair.

Pause.

Jacqui Is your mum OK, now? Is she . . . seeing anyone else, or –

Robin No. She’d never do that. She’s one of those. In it till the very end, type thing.

Jacqui Almost unheard of now.

Robin I’ll always be grateful that . . . for my – that my mum and dad . . . you know. I couldn’t have handled that. With everything else, what with all the other things I felt I had to deal with; that would have pushed me over the edge, I reckon. A broken home.

Jacqui Don’t say that, ‘broken’. Hate that.

Robin Sorry.

Jacqui Broken implies something that needs fixing. They often work. Very well.

Robin Of course. (Beat.) Even a broken home is right twice a day. Eh?

Jacqui That’s a watch.

Robin Yes, I know that.

Jacqui Oh right. Yes. Very good.

Evening. Jacqui’s room. Jacqui on her bed. Reece sitting. They drink hot chocolate. Reece is in overalls – he’s just back from work.

Reece Takes you back, eh?

Jacqui It’s lovely. Thank you.

Reece ’Member when I’d sneak it up to your room? Yours and David’s. Behind Mum’s back. Huh. She’d always be going on about your bloody teeth. ‘That hot chocolate. Ruddy well eats their teeth.’ Well, it ruddy hasn’t, has it? Look at you, early twenties, perfect teeth. Let’s see ’em.

Jacqui smiles a toothy grin.

Reece Yeah, see, perfect smile.

Jacqui How’s everything going with Julie?

Reece Oh fine, you know. We’re just friends.

Jacqui It’s all right, Dad.

Reece We are, we’re just friends. Can’t a fella be friends with a woman and they just be friends?

Jacqui You should invite her round more.

Reece (shrugs) I dunno.

Jacqui Why don’t you invite her round tonight?

Reece Nah, she’s at pilates or yogalates or whichever one it is, Wednesdays. Thing I don’t understand is, they all go out on the piss after, and sometimes a bag of bloody chips. One cancels out the other, she says. It’s like ‘offsetting’. Leaves me thinking, well why are you spending all that money to end up staying exactly the bloody same? Don’t ask me, Jacqui love, I don’t ask myself.

Sips his drink.

Christmas, this always reminds me of. Christmas Eve. What were them Plasticine things we used to watch? ’Round Christmas time. That dog, with the –
